7. **Leases and E21-9's renewal rule.** A lease lasts 24 hours and is renewed every hour
   (infrastructure §5.2's Proposed defaults), capped by the backend's own expiry. `LeaseKeeper`
   applies the rule:

   | Event | Effect on the lease |
   |---|---|
   | A renewal fails with an outage | Kept until it expires |
   | Any other refusal (the token deleted, the policy changed, tampering) | Dropped at once: that is a revocation |
   | A renewal for another connection | Dropped |
   | The lease reaches its expiry | Dropped |
   | A renewal after the lease was dropped | Brings nothing back |

   An executor's OpenBao token lives at most 30 days through renewals. Logging in again past that
   is V3's work.

### Residuals, disclosed

- The connection manager holds each grant's secret id until it hands it to the exchange it
  starts, so a compromised manager could log in as that connection's exchange for 10 minutes:
  read its code and the client secret, and write its token. It can read no token. This follows
  from infrastructure §5.2's grant; a response-wrapped grant would close it and is follow-up.
- `provision` leaves the policies and roles of a removed connection in place, and each run
  issues new secret ids without revoking the old ones. The cell's deployment manager (D1) owns
  that cleanup.

- A secret from OpenBao passes through an unzeroized `serde_json` string on the way to its
  `secrecy` box; only the process that may read it holds it.
- No vault operation is journaled yet (DEC-976 item 6). OpenBao's own audit device is the cell's
  (K1); the journal event comes with its spec change.
- No metric exists yet, so the canary scan covers `Debug` output, errors, and refusals only.

### Not in this decision

Wiring the executor to its lease (V3, E21-9), the cell's OpenBao chart and its storage (K1, S2),
and the key-permission checks at executor start (E21-9).

### Alternatives considered

- **One identity for every process, checked in code.** Rejected: the vault itself must refuse,
  or a compromised process reads everything.
- **Wildcard paths (`connections/+/token`) in the API's and the manager's policies.** Rejected
  by E21-32's "no wildcard": the policies are regenerated per connection.
- **Kubernetes auth now.** Deferred to K1. It ties the backend to the cluster, and the local and
  Compose cells have none.
- **Auto-unseal now.** It is a spend (H4), so the founder decides it.

<a id="dec-980"></a>

## DEC-980: Metrics through the OpenTelemetry API with a Prometheus pull exporter, a closed catalog, and a linted scrape

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The agent, under DEC-79 and DEC-176 (reversible engineering choices); the dependency kind was pre-approved by the founder as D-2 ([DEC-990](earlier-900-1999.md#dec-990)) |
| **Story or claim** | E21-3, lane O1 of the [managed-cells plan](../tasks/managed-cells.md); claim #1440 |

### Decision

1. **A new crate, `mandate-telemetry`, not safety-critical and not a core crate.** It sits at layer
   8 with no workspace dependency. Its `allowed_dependents` in `xtask/layers.toml` names only the
   process binaries that host a cycle (`mandate-agentd` today; `mandate-process` or the daemon
   crates of lane P are added when they take it), so no core crate can record or read a metric:
   this is OPS-11's layering check. `opentelemetry`, `opentelemetry_sdk`,
   `opentelemetry-prometheus` and `prometheus` join `impure_crates`, so no pure crate can take
   them directly either.
2. **The D-2 rows** (DEC-990): `opentelemetry` and `opentelemetry_sdk` `=0.33.0` with only the
   `metrics` feature, `opentelemetry-prometheus` `=0.33.0` without default features, and
   `prometheus` `=0.14.0` without default features, so no protobuf crate is built. 0.33.1 was
   released 2026-10-09, under two weeks ago, so 0.33.0 (2026-09-18) is pinned. Two crates are new
   to the lockfile besides the four: `futures-macro` and `lazy_static`.
3. **The pull endpoint is written on `std::net`, not on an HTTP server crate.** `GET /metrics`
   answers the exposition text; anything else is `404 not_found`. One thread, a request line
   read to at most 1024 bytes, and one second for a scraper's read and write, as
   `mandate-agentd`'s health listener does. No D-1 crate is needed for it, and nothing in it
   connects outward, so it runs air-gapped with no collector (design §8.1).
4. **The metric set is closed and typed.** A `Metric` has private fields and is built only in
   `catalog.rs`; `CATALOG` is every metric. Label keys are the six of design §8.1 (`workspace`,
   `agent`, `account_ref`, `process_type`, `reason_code`, `release`), each taking only its own
   value type: an opaque ID (1 to 64 of `[a-z0-9_]`, starting with a letter, DEC-710 item 4's
   shape), a `ProcessType`, a `ReasonCode` (only `&'static str`, so no runtime value becomes
   one), or a `Release` (never a bare number). Every value a metric carries is a count or a 0 or 1
   state; a duration kind is added with the first metric that needs one.
5. **The label lint is two checks, both run in CI.** `lint::catalog` checks each metric's name
   (`mandate_` and `[a-z0-9_]`), its suffix against its kind, its help text, and a list of
   forbidden words (order details, positions, money, mandate content, people, secrets);
   `lint::exposition` checks a scrape line by line against the catalog. Every scrape is linted
   before it is served, and one the lint finds anything in is answered
   `503 telemetry_unavailable`, never sent. A record whose labels are not its metric's declared
   keys, or offered to a metric of another kind, is dropped and counted in
   `mandate_telemetry_refused_total` by reason.
6. **Telemetry failing changes nothing** (OPS-11). Every record returns nothing and cannot fail.
   `Metrics::off` records and serves nothing, and a process whose metrics cannot be built runs
   with it. An exporter that cannot bind or start its thread is an error the process carries on
   past. The process's announcement lines ignore their own write errors.
7. **`mandate-agentd` counts its host's calls.** `Instrumented` wraps the `Host`, calls through
   unchanged, and after each call returns counts it by outcome (`completed`, `failed`) in
   `mandate_process_reconciliations_total`, `mandate_process_cycles_total` and
   `mandate_process_settles_total`, labeled `process_type="agent_process"`. The binary serves the
   exporter on an ephemeral loopback port and prints `metrics <addr>` after `health <addr>`.
   Binding to the pod address for the cell's Prometheus is the chart's choice (E21-36, E21-39).
8. **Disclosed limit.** The release profile aborts on panic (ES-09), so a panic inside the
   OpenTelemetry or Prometheus crates on the exporter thread would end the process, which then
   recovers from the journal as after any crash (design §3.4). The crate's own code is under the
   same no-panic lints as the trading paths, and its thread is started through
   `thread::Builder`, whose failure is an error rather than a panic.

### Rationale

Design §8.1 and ES-18 already chose the OpenTelemetry API with a Prometheus pull exporter, so the
choice here is how to keep it from leaking strategy and from touching a decision. Making the
catalog and the label keys unrepresentable outside their set (the trust ladder's first rung)
leaves the lint to check what types cannot: the words in names and help text, and the bytes that
actually leave. Linting every scrape before it is served means a defect in the exporter crates,
such as an added scope label or `target_info`, is refused rather than sent. Recording after the
call returns, through a wrapper, keeps the decision cycle's code free of telemetry.

### Alternatives considered

- **The `prometheus` or `prometheus-client` crate alone, without OpenTelemetry:** fewer crates,
  but ES-18 names the OpenTelemetry API so that OTLP push (M8) is an exporter change, not a
  rewrite.
- **Serving `/metrics` from an HTTP server crate (D-1):** no other part of this lane needs one,
  and a GET-only endpoint is about forty lines on `std::net`.
- **A label allowlist checked only in a test over the registry:** catches what tests record, not
  what production records; the typed labels and the served-scrape lint cover both.
- **Making `mandate-telemetry` safety-critical:** the plan names it not a core crate; what is
  safety-critical is that it cannot reach a decision, which the layering rule holds.

<a id="dec-982"></a>

## DEC-982: `cp-agent`'s inbound checks: a signed envelope over the canonical body, one pinned Ed25519 key per kind, and a port it declares

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering readings under DEC-79, taken under DEC-176; each only refuses more than design CP-5 requires, and none adds an effect) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder, managed-cells lane G0), under DEC-79, DEC-176, DEC-440 items 3 and 5, and DEC-921 |
| **Story or claim** | E20-3; plan DEC-967 lane G0; claim [#1436](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1436) |

Control-plane design §3 and CP-5 say that `cp-agent` verifies each inbound instruction against a
key pinned in the installed release, never the TLS channel, and hands it to workspace control
services, which journal it (journal spec §9.17, [DEC-981](DEC-981.md)) before it takes effect.
The design names no wire form, signature scheme, or interface. These are the readings this lane
takes in `mandate-cp-agent`.

### Decision

1. **The wire form.** An inbound message is one JSON object of at most `kind`, `key_id`, `body`, and
   `signature`, received in journal spec §4's canonical form (RFC 8785) and at most 64 KiB. A
   message in any other form, however its signature reads, is refused `malformed`: one document
   then has exactly one digest, so a replay re-spaced or reordered can neither dodge §9.17's
   de-duplication nor take the genuine message's sequence (the review of #1467). `kind` is one of
   `license`, `release_manifest`, `release_withdrawn`, `catalog_entry`, `data_bundle`; anything
   else, including every outbound kind and `receipt`, is refused `out_of_list` with `message:
   unknown` and no sequence (journal spec rule 142).
2. **What is signed.** The signature is Ed25519 over `mandate-cp/1 ‖ 0x00 ‖ kind ‖ 0x00 ‖ key_id ‖
   0x00 ‖ canonical(body)` (journal spec §4's canonical JSON), as 128 lowercase hex characters.
   Binding the kind and the key ID means a signature for one kind never verifies as another, even
   under the same key (a withdrawal relabeled as a manifest is `bad_signature`).
3. **One pinned key per kind.** The installed release pins `(key_id, kind, public key)` for the four
   offline keys of DEC-440 item 5: license, release, registry, data. A license verifies only under a
   license key, a manifest or withdrawal only under a release key, a data bundle only under a data
   key, and a catalog entry under the release key when its `kind` is `connector_package` and the
   registry key when it is `model_registry_entry` (design §3.8 allows either; this is the tighter
   reading). A key that is not pinned, or pinned for another kind, is `unknown_key`. A release pins
   each key ID once and only a 32-byte key; `pin` refuses anything else.
4. **The order of checks** fixes which reason is recorded: over 64 KiB or not JSON → `malformed`;
   kind outside the list → `out_of_list`; not canonical, or an envelope member outside the four →
   `malformed`; no signature → `unsigned`; key → `unknown_key`; signature → `bad_signature`; body
   outside its closed schema → `malformed`; a license that does not name this deployment →
   `not_addressed`. Whether its organization has a workspace here is workspace services' check,
   since a managed cell hosts many organizations. The body is parsed with the envelope, but only
   two of its members are read before the signature verifies: `kind`, to choose a catalog
   entry's key (a tampered `kind` then fails the signature), and `sequence`, recorded on a refusal
   when it is an integer of at least 1, so a refused replay is auditable; it is never trusted. The
   body's schema is judged only after the signature verifies.
5. **Closed bodies.** Each body has exactly the members journal spec §9.17 records (a manifest adds
   its image digests, at least one, by component, strictly ascending), with §9.17's rules 138 and
   139 applied before hand-over, so a verified instruction always maps onto a record `append`
   accepts. Integers are bounded by the canonical form, at most 2^53 − 1; a consumer doing date
   arithmetic with `grace_days` uses checked arithmetic.
6. **The port.** `cp-agent` declares `InstructionSink` with `hand_over(Verified)` and
   `refused(Refusal)`; workspace control services implement it. `Verified` has private fields and
   only `receive` builds one, so they can never be handed an unverified instruction (rung 1 of the
   trust ladder). The answer is `Journaled`, `AlreadyApplied` (the digest in effect, DEC-981 item
   5), `Refused` (CP-9's `replayed`), or `Unavailable` (nothing journaled, nothing applied; `cp-agent`
   keeps the message). Anti-rollback is the implementer's (DEC-981 item 4).
7. **Layering.** `mandate-cp-agent` is a pure, safety-critical layer-1 crate depending on
   `mandate-canon`, `mandate-time`, and `ring` (already registered, verification only outside its
   tests). Its `forbidden_internal` list names the journal crates, the mandate registry
   (`mandate-spec`), the runtime, the executor, the connections and vault crates, and the process
   crates, so it can never append, hold a writer epoch, place an order, or read a credential (CP-4).

### Rationale

Ed25519 is what `ring` already verifies for the passkey and token checks, so the crate adds no
dependency; a detached signature over our own canonical bytes avoids pulling in a JOSE or COSE
library. Domain separation by kind and key is the cheapest defense against a confused-deputy
replay across kinds. Requiring the canonical form costs one serialization and makes the digest a
document's identity. The size cap bounds what unauthenticated input can cost before the signature
is checked.

### Alternatives considered

- **JWS (or COSE) documents.** Standard, but needs a new dependency or a hand-rolled base64url and
  header parser, and its algorithm field is a known foot-gun. Rejected for v1.
- **Accept either release or registry key for any catalog entry** (design §3.8's literal "Release or
  registry key"). Looser than needed; rejected for the per-kind binding.
- **Verify in workspace services.** Would put the link's parser inside the journal writer's
  process boundary; the design puts it in `cp-agent`.

<a id="dec-986"></a>

## DEC-986: Workspace services and agent processes meet only in the journal, checked by the layers

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of claim [#1437](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1437)), under DEC-79 (reversible engineering) and DEC-176 (it only adds a refusal) |
| **Story or claim** | E21-42 (lane M1 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967); claim #1437; infrastructure design §3.1; journal spec §2; DEC-17 |

### Context

E21-42 asks that workspace services and an agent's processes meet only in the journal, so the API,
the deployment manager, the approval service or `cp-agent` can never act on a runtime, an executor
or the scheduler behind the journal's back. Until now that held by convention and by a few
`forbidden_internal` lists (the CLI's and the dispatcher's), each written for its own reason. This
decision puts the rule on the "checked" rung for the crates that exist now.

### Decision

1. **Two process groups in `xtask/layers.toml`.** A `[process_groups]` table names the
   `workspace_services` crates and the `agent_process` crates. `cargo xtask layers` (part of
   `cargo xtask ci fast`'s lint) refuses any crate of one group that reaches a crate of the other,
   directly or through any chain of path dependencies of any kind (normal, dev and build), with
   the message "workspace services and agent processes meet only in the journal". Dev-dependencies
   count, as they do for `forbidden_internal` (DEC-525): a test that needs both sides belongs in a
   tool or harness crate, which is in neither group.
2. **The groups today.**
   - `workspace_services`: `mandate-api` (the workspace API contract), `mandate-audit` (the audit
     reads the API serves), `mandate-cli` (Phase 1's workspace control services, infrastructure
     design §3.1), `mandate-dispatcher` (the notification process), and the planned
     `mandate-cp-agent` (control-plane design §3, CP-4) and `mandate-cp-site` (the control
     services' handler behind `cp-agent`'s port, which appends its records), both lane G1's.
     The global control-plane service, `mandate-cp`, runs outside every workspace deployment, so
     it is in neither group; lane G1 keeps it from agent processes and journal writers with its
     own `forbidden_internal` list.
   - `agent_process`: `mandate-agentd` (the long-running agent process, DEC-968), `mandate-shell`
     (the process shell that hosts the runtime and executor stages), `mandate-paper` (the runner),
     `mandate-executor`, the connectors `mandate-alpaca` and `mandate-robinhood`, and
     `mandate-mcp` (the MCP client only the connectors use).
3. **Shared crates are in neither group.** The journal and its stores, the pure cores (domain,
   accounting, risk, spec, builder, approval, identity, notify), connections, artifacts, the vault,
   market data, and two pure decision libraries: `mandate-runtime` and `mandate-modelhost`. Both
   hold functions over values, no loop, no clock and no I/O, so linking them gives a service no
   channel to a running agent process; what runs them (`mandate-shell`, `mandate-agentd`,
   `mandate-paper`) is in the agent group. The CLI uses `mandate_modelhost::content()` to register a
   model's content hash (DEC-525), and the model host's output type lives in `mandate-runtime`, so
   putting either in the agent group would refuse that existing, intended dependency. Tool and
   harness crates (`xtask`, `mandate-refcases`, `mandate-rh-sim`, `mandate-shell-rh-sim`,
   `mandate-identity-testkit`) are in neither group.
4. **Every name is checked.** A group name that is neither a workspace member nor in `planned` is a
   problem, so a typo leaves nothing unguarded; a crate in both groups is a problem. A new crate
   that is a workspace service or an agent process joins its list in the change that creates it
   (the deployment manager, the approval service, the scheduler, the data service when lane M2
   decides its side; `mandate-cp-agent` and `mandate-cp-site` leave `planned` when they are created).
5. **The channels that remain** are listed in infrastructure design §3.1: the journal, the
   host-local market-data channel (runtimes and executors only), and the orchestrator's probes.
6. **Deferred, and why.** E21-42's other criteria need pieces other lanes build: the chart's
   default-deny `NetworkPolicy` showing no route from a workspace-services pod to an agent-process
   pod waits on K1's chart (E21-36); the fault-injection test that drops every `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY`
   wake-up and still delivers each owner command, a kill switch included, by tailing waits on the
   long-running processes (E21-2 and the process lanes). Until then the story is not done.

### Rationale

A crate-graph rule is the highest rung that can hold this today (AGENTS.md trust ladder): two
processes can only call each other in-process if one links the other's code, and the closure the
layers check already computes (DEC-525) sees every chain. Making the groups data keeps the rule
one table that a new crate's author edits, beside the layer it already has to declare.

### Alternatives considered

- **More `forbidden_internal` lists, one per service crate.** Each list would have to name every
  agent-process crate and be kept in step as crates are added; a missed name is silent. One pair
  of groups states the rule once.
- **Put `mandate-runtime` in the agent group.** Stricter, but it refuses the CLI's model
  registration through the model host today, and the runtime crate holds no process loop. If a
  loop ever lands in it, it moves to the agent group and the CLI's dependency moves to a shared
  crate first.
- **Leave the CLI out of the workspace group as a founder-only tool.** It is the control services
  in Phase 1 (infrastructure design §3.1); it already cannot reach an order path (DEC-525), and the
  group adds the executor, the connectors and the agent process to what it can never reach.

<a id="dec-987"></a>

## DEC-987: The beta form is closed in a production build until Supabase is configured

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | An agent (Claude Code, lane W1 builder), under DEC-176: a reversible product-behaviour choice with no spending, credential, legal or compliance text |
| **Story or claim** | E21-38, lane W1, claim [#1447](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1447); revises [DEC-731](DEC-731.md) item 4 |

### Decision

1. **Open or closed.** `betaOpen` in `web/src/lib/beta.ts` decides. The form is always open in
   development, the tests and the e2e builds, which keep a local file or stub the route. In any
   other build, it is open only once both `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL` and
   `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` are set.
2. **Closed, the form shows a status and sends nothing.** It says "The private beta isn't taking
   requests yet. Check back soon." in place of the fields. The route answers 403 with
   `{"error":"closed"}` before reading the body. It writes nothing to Supabase or to a file. A form
   that meets that answer turns closed as well.
3. **Open, nothing changes.** DEC-731 item 4 still holds: a missing table or another Supabase error
   answers 503, and only development and tests fall back to the local file.

### Rationale

The demo may run before Supabase is set up. Without this, every request in a production build
would fail with 503 and a "try again" message that never comes true. A closed form is honest and
writes nothing. Setting the two public variables opens it at the next deploy, with no code change.

### Alternatives considered

- **Leave the 503.** The demo would show an error.
- **Hide the form.** That changes the landing page's layout and its tests. The closed status keeps
  the layout.
- **Store requests somewhere else** (Workers KV, R2): new bindings, which DEC-731 already turned
  down.

<a id="dec-990"></a>

## DEC-990: The managed-cell dependencies are pre-approved as kinds, cell #1 is hosted for the demo once the drills first pass, and the cell lanes may use the one-PR flow

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (founder-reserved, DEC-79: spending, a narrowed gate, and an extension of the founder's DEC-921). The founder confirmed all three items in their own words on [PR #1431](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/1431#issuecomment-6229858310) on 2026-10-17 at 04:53 UTC: "I approve DEC-990 items 1, 2 and 3: D-1 to D-7 pre-approved as kinds, cell #1 hosted for the demo once every C1 drill has passed once on main (paper and demo only), and the managed-cell and demo lanes on DEC-921’s one-PR flow." |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The founder. Items 1 and 2 first answered in the coordinator's session at about 04:40 UTC on 2026-10-17, by multiple choice, and item 3 proposed by the coordinator; all three confirmed by the founder on PR #1431. Recorded by an agent (Claude Code, the managed-cells planning lane). The number was handed out by the coordinator after DEC-969 turned out to be taken by #1430 |
| **Story or claim** | The [managed-cells plan](../tasks/managed-cells.md) ("Dependencies the founder approves", "Order", H1, H3); backlog E21-28 to E21-50; [DEC-967](earlier-900-1999.md#dec-967) item 16; [DEC-921](earlier-900-1999.md#dec-921); DEC-77; DEC-822 |

### Context

DEC-967 item 16 approves the two Hetzner VMs and Cloudflare R2, effective when every local-cell
drill passes in CI on `main`. The managed-cells plan, as its own tightening, added that the drills
keep passing for a week of merges before anything is bought; DEC-967 does not say that. The 2026-10-26 demo is nine days away. The
plan also lists seven kinds of dependency (D-1 to D-7) whose `docs/dependencies.md` rows the founder
approves, one by one, as each lane first needs them. The coordinator asked the founder about both
and relayed the answers below.

| Question (as relayed) | The founder's answer, as relayed |
|---|---|
| Dependencies D-1 to D-7 | "Pre-approve D-1 to D-7 (Recommended): You approve all seven now. Builders add each row in the PR that first uses it, I check the row matches the kind you approved, and I merge it, so no builder waits on you. Anything outside these seven kinds still comes to you." |
| Does the 2026-10-26 demo run on the DEC-822 CX22 or on the managed cell? | "Rush the hosted cell": host cell #1 for the demo without the week-of-drills local-cell gate |

### Decision

1. **D-1 to D-7 are pre-approved as kinds** (Accepted, founder). The seven kinds are those the
   plan lists: an async HTTP server crate (D-1); the OpenTelemetry metrics API and a Prometheus
   exporter (D-2); a Kubernetes API client, or plain HTTPS through the existing `reqwest` and
   `rustls` (D-3); the CI tools `k3d` or `kind`, `helm` and `kubeconform`, pinned in
   `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and `.cursor/install.sh` together (D-4); the CloudNativePG operator
   and chart, and container images for PostgreSQL 18, OpenBao, Prometheus, Alertmanager,
   `cloudflared` and k3s, each pinned by digest (D-5); the Workers adapter for Next.js and its CLI
   (D-6); and `cosign`, an SBOM generator and `cargo-auditable` (D-7). A builder adds each row in
   the PR that first uses it, with the alternatives considered and the licence. The coordinator
   checks that the row matches its kind and merges it. Anything outside these seven kinds still goes to the founder;
   so, as the recording agent's tightening (DEC-176), do a second crate for the same kind and a
   licence the registry does not already hold. `cargo xtask deps` and the `allowed_external`
   lists of `xtask/layers.toml` keep applying.
2. **Cell #1 is hosted for the demo once every drill has first passed** (Accepted, founder). The
   plan's own week of green merges (its tightening of DEC-967 item 16, not part of that item) is
   dropped for cell #1's hosting, which brings the gate back to exactly what item 16 says, for the
   demo. What the recording
   agent reads into it, each only tightening (DEC-176):
   - every C1 drill (E21-37) must still pass in CI on `main` at least once before anything is
     hosted;
   - the hosted cell carries paper and demo data only;
   - the founder buys H1 (the two Ashburn VMs) and H3 (R2) when the drills first pass, not after
     a week;
   - S1's check against R2 (E21-40) still runs before anything is stored there;
   - live orders from the cell (B3, E21-48) keep their own conditions unchanged: this item hosts
     nothing live.
3. **The managed-cell lanes and the demo lanes may use DEC-921's one-PR flow** (Accepted,
   founder). Every managed-cell story (backlog E21-28 to E21-50) and the demo lanes (the W2 web
   client and the identity bootstrap) may land tests and implementation in one PR: the tests
   committed first and shown failing on the stubs or on a seeded bug, then the implementation in
   the same PR. ES-22's spec-first split, the independent review on a different model, zero
   missed mutants on safety-critical crates, green `fast` and `full`, and every other
   safety-critical rule stay as they are. **Why it is not agent-accepted:** the coordinator
   relayed this item as accepted by an agent, as reversible process (DEC-79). But DEC-921 is the
   founder's, and its item 3 says "Rows off the R0 path keep DEC-77 unchanged. This decision ends
   when R0's evidence PR merges." Extending it changes a founder decision and relaxes the DEC-77
   order on safety-critical paths, so `AGENTS.md` rule 9 and DEC-79 leave it to the founder. The
   founder confirmed it on #1431, so the lanes may use the one-PR flow.

### Rationale

Pre-approving the dependency kinds keeps the founder's say over what enters the supply chain while
removing a wait from each lane's first PR; the seven kinds are already the plan's named choices.
Hosting the cell for the demo trades the plan's week of green merges for time, while keeping the
one thing that week was guarding: every drill has passed at least once on `main`, and nothing live is
hosted. The one-PR flow halves the review cycles per story; the safety comes from the tests
existing, failing first and being reviewed, which it keeps (DEC-921's own rationale).

### Alternatives considered

- **Approve each dependency row as it comes** (DEC-967 as written): every lane's first PR waits on
  the founder.
- **Run the demo on the DEC-822 CX22** (offered; not chosen, as relayed): the demo would show the
  single-VM path the plan now treats as a dev convenience.
- **Keep the week of green merges and host after the demo:** safest, but misses the demo.
- **Accept item 3 as an agent decision** (as relayed): not taken, for the reason in item 3.

### To confirm

The founder confirms by posting on PR #1431, in their own words, for example:

> I approve DEC-990 items 1, 2 and 3: D-1 to D-7 pre-approved as kinds, cell #1 hosted for the demo once every C1 drill has passed once on main (paper and demo only), and the managed-cell and demo lanes on DEC-921's one-PR flow.

Any item left out stays Proposed.

<a id="dec-991"></a>

## DEC-991: The founding grant is written once, by `mandate workspace found`, through the one membership writer, deciding inside the append's attempt

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering readings under DEC-79 and DEC-176; each only refuses or narrows, and none loosens a rule) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of claim [#1445](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1445)), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | Lane ID1 (E9-7 follow-up, demo blocker for A1, #1444); claim #1445; identity spec §3.2, §4.3, ID-13; journal spec §9.12 |

### Context

The workspace API server authenticates the founder and then refuses every workspace route with
`no_membership`: no binary writes the founding `MemberActivated` that journal spec §9.12 and
identity spec §3.2 describe, and `append` refused every `Member*` type as `unknown_event_type`.

### Decision

1. **The journal admits `MemberActivated` alone.** `mandate-journal` registers `MemberActivated`
   at schema version 1 on the control stream, with rules 96, 98 to 100, 103, 105, and 106, tested
   against every `MemberActivated` case of the `membership` vectors. The other six `Member*` types
   stay `unknown_event_type` until E9-7 (#760) registers them. Admitting one type of seven only
   narrows what the journal accepts.
2. **The writer is the `control_services` system.** The grant's envelope is written by the same
   `system` actor that opens the control stream (the vectors' `founding` draft), in the stream's own
   environment, read from its `StreamOpened`. Its event id is DEC-290's derivation over the member
   at the head it is appended at. The CLI's private `Writer` gains `Founder`, which writes
   `MemberActivated` and nothing else.
3. **Only a new workspace is founded.** The grant commits only on a control stream that holds
   its `StreamOpened` and nothing else (`workspace_not_new` otherwise, `workspace_not_open` on an
   empty one). So its `independent_approval_required: false` is the workspace's effective policy
   (mandate spec §4.3: the platform and retail profiles do not require it, and no `PolicyChanged`
   or `WorkspaceProfileAssigned` can have preceded it). The spec only asks for the grant at
   creation; this reading is the strict one.
4. **One mapping from a stored envelope to a membership record.** `mandate_identity::control_entry`
   maps each of the seven `Member*` envelopes to its `MembershipRecord` and any other record to
   `ControlEntry::Other`. A membership record that does not map is an error, never skipped. The
   seal stays inside `mandate-identity`. The workspace store's adapters (A1's lookup among them)
   use this function rather than their own reading. `control_entry` is public, so any crate can
   build a `MembershipRecord` from a value. Only a holder of the seal can fold records
   (`MembershipFold::new` takes the `Seal`), so a record built this way grants nothing. A later
   slice may narrow the input to a stored event.
5. **The decision is made inside the writer's attempt.** `write_membership`'s loop becomes
   `write_decided`, which asks its caller what to commit from the view each attempt read, at the
   instant it stamps. `found_workspace` decides there, so a moved head re-runs the decision. Two
   bootstraps racing on one stream cannot both grant: the second re-reads and finds the first's
   grant. `write_membership` is unchanged in behaviour.
6. **Idempotent, for a Kubernetes Job.** When the stream's first membership record is this
   member's founding grant, written exactly as `founding_grant` writes it, a run commits nothing,
   prints that the workspace is already founded, and exits 0. Any other first membership record
   refuses it with `members_exist` and a non-zero exit. A fence, an unavailable journal, or an
   answer lost after the commit is read as a moved head, after a backoff. The re-read then finds a
   grant that did commit, so nothing is written twice.
7. **No more than the owner bundle.** The roles are `OWNER_BUNDLE` (workspace admin, operator,
   approver; identity spec §4.3), never chosen: the command takes no role. The member is a ULID
   the operator passes (`--member`), so a Job's retries name the same principal. The command never
   takes a name, an address, or an identity-provider subject. The sign-in method is required, with
   no default. `session_ref` is null, since the system writes without a session (rule 105). A
   re-run that names another method for the same member still reads as already founded: the
   method records how the founder signed in and grants nothing.
8. **The owner bundle is the retail one** (identity spec §3.2, §4.3). A workspace whose
   organization is not retail would need its founding roles and policy read from its profile.
   Item 3 refuses any workspace with a record before its founding grant, so no profile can have
   been assigned yet. That reading is owed with the organization story (E9-2).

A Kubernetes Job runs it as `mandate workspace found --workspace <id> --member <ULID> --method
oidc --journal <DSN without a password> --store <dir>`, with `PGPASSWORD` from the Job's secret.
It needs `restartPolicy: OnFailure` and no other state. The Helm chart (lane K1, #1432) owns the
manifest.

### Rationale

The founder needs one membership before any route serves. Writing it through the one writer
(DEC-646) keeps the order rule and the seal in one place. Deciding inside the attempt is what
makes "never two founders" hold under concurrency, not only sequentially.

### Alternatives considered

- **Check for members before calling `write_membership`.** Refused: two concurrent runs both pass
  the check, and the second's moved-head retry re-runs only the order guard, so it would commit a
  second founding grant.
- **Mint the member's ULID inside the command.** Refused: a Job retry would mint another one and
  then be refused, though the first run's grant committed.
- **Register all seven `Member*` types now.** That is E9-7's claim, and the founding grant needs
  only one.

<a id="dec-992"></a>

## DEC-992: `mandate identity link` writes the API server's subject-link file: the subject from standard input, only its digest written, the file replaced whole

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering readings under DEC-79 and DEC-176; each only refuses or narrows). Whether a hashed subject in a host file is acceptable beyond the demo cell stays DEC-978 item 7's founder question |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of claim [#1445](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1445)), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | Lane ID1; claim #1445; workspace API spec §3.11 item 7 and DEC-978 item 7 (PR #1446); identity spec §3.1, §3.3 |

### Decision

1. **The format is §3.11 item 7's, written one way.** Each line is `sha256:`, 64 lowercase hex
   digits of the SHA-256 of the canonical `{"issuer","subject"}` object (journal spec §4), one
   space, the principal's ULID, and `\n`. Lines are ascending by digest, and no digest appears
   twice.
2. **The subject never leaves standard input.** The command takes `--issuer`, `--member` and
   `--links`, but no subject argument, so no process listing or shell history shows the subject.
   Neither the file, the output nor any error carries it. One trailing `\n` or `\r\n` is stripped.
   A subject that is empty, holds a control character, or is longer than 1024 bytes (the line end
   not counted) is refused, and so is an issuer that is empty or holds whitespace. The issuer is
   taken exactly, with no normalization, because the server compares the token's `iss` exactly.
3. **Idempotent, and a subject is never re-pointed.** A link already there writes nothing and
   succeeds. A digest linked to another principal is refused `subject_linked_elsewhere`.
   Removing or re-pointing a link is left to a later slice; until then, an operator edits the file.
4. **Never extend a file the server would refuse.** An existing file that is not a regular file
   (a symlink, a directory), that is group- or world-writable, or that is owned by neither this
   process's user nor root is refused `links_unsafe`. A file that does not parse as item 1 is
   refused `links_malformed`. Nothing is changed in either case.
5. **Replaced whole.** The new text goes to `<links>.writing`, created exclusively with mode `0644`.
   It is synced, renamed over the file, and the directory is synced, so the server never reads a
   partial file. A second writer at once finds the staging file and is refused `links_busy`.
   After a crash the operator removes the stale staging file. The file is `0644`, not `0600`,
   because it is not a secret (DEC-978 item 7) and the server may run as another user than the
   writer. The file is owned by whoever runs the command, and the server accepts only its own user
   or root. So the command must run as root or as the server's user: a run as any other user
   writes a file the server refuses, and every sign-in then fails closed. A line with a `\r` is
   malformed, so a file edited by hand with CRLF line ends is refused, not silently rewritten.

6. **The file's path defaults to the server's.** The API server reads the subject-link file
   only at `/etc/mandate/subject-links` ([DEC-1023](DEC-1023.md) item 1), so `--links` defaults
   to that path. The flag stays so tests and a staging copy can name another file; a file written
   anywhere else is not one the server reads.

### Rationale

The spec fixes what the server reads. These readings fix how one writer produces exactly that, and
keep the raw subject out of every place a log or listing could show it.

### Alternatives considered

- **The subject as an argument:** refused, because process listings and shell histories would
  show it.
- **Rewrite in place:** refused, because a reader could see a half-written file.

<a id="dec-996"></a>

## DEC-996: The harness research loop's first slice: mandate-research-run, the runtime's research inputs, and the provisional records behind the §6.2 preconditions

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent; reversible engineering readings under DEC-79 and DEC-176, each of which tightens or closes a gap by the reading that adds no risk); items 4, 5, 7 and 9 superseded in part by [DEC-1120](earlier-900-1999.md#dec-1120) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | An agent (Claude Code, intelligence lane I2), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E19-2, E19-3, E17-2's harness half, and the judging half of E19-4; [claim #1462](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1462) |

### Context

The [agent harness spec](../../specs/agent-harness.md) v0.1 describes the research loop (§5.3, §5.4,
§6.2 to §6.5, §7) and the core's new inputs (§10.3). Three things it needs do not exist yet: the
closed agent-stream `ModelInvocationRecorded` and the candidates' verdict record (E15-8), and the
invalidation verdict (E19-5). The spec says no run starts until they do (§6.2 preconditions). The
model gateway (lane I1) and the news and filings readers (lane I3) are being built in parallel, and
the shell's run path is on the R0 path, which this lane may not touch. This decision records how
the first slice is built within those limits.

### Decision

1. **Where the code lives.** `mandate-research-run` (layer 5, safety-critical, pure; DEC-431 item
   20) holds the worker: the retrieval plan, the read-only reader ports, the gateway port, the
   capture records, the call, `settle`, the error-kind treatments, and the strict output parse. It
   cannot name `IntentSink` (HI-1), and its `forbidden_internal` list in `xtask/layers.toml` keeps
   it off every journal crate and the artifact store, which `cargo xtask layers` checks (HI-16;
   E19-3's "an xtask check that it depends on no journal crate"). The harness checks, the
   platform's fill, admission, and the run schedule live in `mandate-runtime` (layer 6): the spec
   puts judging in the core (§6.2 step 5), and a layer-5 crate cannot depend on `mandate-research`,
   which is also layer 5.
2. **A second entry point, no new effect.** The core's research inputs arrive as
   `Input::Research` through `handle_research`, which shares `handle`'s batch, append-doubt, and
   retry rules and returns its effects plus `ResearchRequest`s (start, call, cancel). `handle`
   refuses `Input::Research` (`not_interpreted`), so the trading loop never waits on research
   (HI-7). No `Effect` variant is added: the shell's run path matches `Effect` exhaustively and is
   on the R0 path. A research batch whose append is unresolved blocks every input, the kill switch
   included, exactly as a trading batch does (journal spec §5.1), and `handle` cannot retry it, so
   the shell resolves it by handing `handle_research` the same research input again, at once, before
   anything else. The order inside a step is a contract the shell keeps: it appends the step's
   drafts and has them committed before it hands any `ResearchRequest` to the worker, so a call
   never leaves before the observations it names (HI-2); the types carry the requests apart from the
   effects, and the shell's own test of that order belongs to the hook below. **The run-path hook
   for after R0:** the shell's research driver, a task that
   calls `handle_research(Poll)` on each tick, stores each item's record and the reply's artifacts
   before handing them in, runs the worker against `mandate-marketdata`'s bars and lane I3's
   readers, calls lane I1's gateway, builds `ResearchContext` from the account stream's working
   universe, the folded lineages and active theses, and the meter stream's reservations, and
   rebuilds the drift fold from the observation artifacts on replay.
3. **The preconditions are read, not assumed.** `Preconditions::read` takes a `RecordCatalog`, and
   `JournalCatalog`, the only production catalogue, registers neither the invalidation verdict nor
   the candidates' verdict record, so no run starts in any deployment until E19-5 and E15-8 change
   its answer. The fields are private, but `RecordCatalog` is a public trait, so this rests on the
   shell passing `JournalCatalog`: a caller that passes another catalogue can open the gate, and the
   shell's hook and its review are what keep that out of production. Tests use a catalogue double. The third precondition is held by the worker: retrieval drops every licensed
   item (`licensed`), so the plan reads only text that may be kept in full.
4. **Provisional records.** Until E15-8 closes them: `ModelInvocationRecorded` (purpose
   `research`) carries `run_id` (in place of the envelope's `correlation_id`, which a draft cannot
   set), `call_id`, the pinned identity, `outcome`, `reported_identity`, `request_digest`,
   `prompt_ref`, `response_ref`, `cost_usd`, `called_at` (the risk clock the answer arrived at, which
   the schedule counts from), `completed_at`, `retrieved_context` (the run's observation ids), and
   `used` (false for a late answer to a cancelled run). The verdicts are their own event,
   `CandidateVerdictsRecorded`: the run, its call record, an outcome (`judged`, `not_pinned`, or
   `output_rejected` with the parser's code), and one line per candidate in output order. The fold
   reads both as inert. Item 3 keeps every one of these drafts out of a production journal; E15-8
   replaces them with its closed schemas.
5. **Run readings.** One poll judges, cancels, or starts, never two of them, so a cancelled run is
   not replaced in the same step. A run starts only in `normal` mode, so `exits_only` starts none
   until E19-5 can journal an invalidation (backlog E19 follow-up minor 9), and a run in flight is
   cancelled when the mode tightens, the pin changes, the research agent is cleared or withdrawn,
   the universe is pinned, research is disallowed, or the wall-clock bound passes. The bound is in
   risk-clock seconds: the start plus the plan's retrieval timeout plus the call deadline rounded up.
   A run whose `ok` call record is drafted waits until that record folds back; it is then judged
   under the version in effect, so a changed pin gives `not_pinned` verdicts and no thesis (HI-17),
   and a mode that left `normal` drops it unjudged. An empty retrieval ends the run with no call.
   The interval counts from the latest of the last call (set when the answer is handled and again
   when its record folds back), the cut-off of the last run that ended with an empty retrieval
   (process-local, so a data plane that answers nothing is not re-read on every poll), and the last
   reservation, at the owner's `behavior.research.interval_s` raised to the policy floor. A run whose
   call record never folds back is given up at its bound. The core checks the answer's call id and
   reported identity against the run's own, so an answer `settle` was skipped for is
   `identity_mismatch` all the same. Run and call ids are
   derived by `IdGen` at ordinals far above any draft's, with a per-process counter, so they never
   repeat within a process or across epochs.
6. **Corroboration until E17-7** (§13 item 3). A thesis is `independent_source` exactly when its
   cited items about its own instrument come from at least two distinct allowlisted text sources;
   otherwise it has none and §8.5 check 15 refuses it. There is no `market_data` corroboration
   until E17-7 states its rule. This only refuses more.
7. **Harness checks.** §6.5's six, in its table's order. `forbidden_language` is a fixed phrase
   list (price targets, targets, objectives, guarantees, risk-free, "will double", "% upside", and
   the like) over the thesis text and its invalidation, case-insensitive with whitespace collapsed;
   it errs toward refusing. **Disclosed residual:** abbreviations ("PT $900") and look-alike
   characters pass it. HI-10 bounds what passes: a thesis cannot size, approve, or place anything,
   and the gateway's output limits are the first layer.
   `duplicate_instrument` counts any earlier candidate that resolved to the same instrument. An
   invalidation is `unknown_thesis`, then `unknown_evidence`, then `accepted`; an accepted one is
   only journaled as a verdict, because the removal it causes is E19-5's, and item 3 keeps runs from
   reaching it meanwhile.
8. **The platform's fill.** `thesis_id` is derived by `IdGen` and a new thesis starts its own
   lineage; a thesis for an instrument with an active thesis is a renewal in that lineage and
   revision. `as_of` is the cut-off and `expires_at` the cut-off plus the horizon. The asset class,
   eligibility, and group come from the run's instrument snapshot; an ineligible instrument or its
   group is added to the admission facts, which only refuses more. `evidence_sources` lists the
   sources of the cited news and filings in the order cited, duplicates kept (journal spec §9.4);
   cited bars are evidence but not sources, since market data needs no allowlist entry and check 14
   would otherwise refuse it. `evidence_ref` is null until the shell stores the evidence artifact
   (item 2's hook). The admitted thesis's `ModelOutputRecorded` cites the cited items'
   observations as `evidence` and the response as `thesis_ref`; checks 1 to 3 journal an ignored
   output, and the other checks' refusals journal the thesis record only.
9. **The output's wire form.** The parse uses the canonical parser, so `conviction` and
   `confidence` are decimal strings in the journal's canonical grammar and a JSON float is
   `not_json`; any unknown member, missing member, or out-of-range value rejects the whole output,
   which is never repaired or partly read. Text and ids may not be blank or hold control
   characters, so every candidate that parses can be filled; one the platform still cannot fill, or
   an admission `mandate_research` cannot decide, ends the run as `output_rejected` with its cause
   in the verdict record, before any thesis record is drafted. Lane I1's output schema is to match; until it lands the
   recorded fake follows this form.
10. **Treatments.** §7.3's table, with two readings: `meter_unavailable` and `input_rejected` alert
    the operator on every occurrence (the spec alerts on repeats; alerting on each is opaque and only
    louder), and the three-in-a-row `deadline_exceeded` alert fires on the third and resets the
    count. Every alert carries only the call record's id and a fixed key (`AGENTS.md` rule 6).
11. **Retrieval readings.** Every reader's answer is filtered again in the worker (knowledge time,
    window, allowlist, licence, scope, duplicates) before the cap, so a reader that leaks cannot
    show the model more (HI-13). A failed read is an empty read. Links are cut to 512 bytes and
    filing forms to 32; bars are one per second; a reader id under the platform's own prefixes
    (`bar:`, `instrument:`) is dropped `reserved_id`. The observation's `instrument_id`
    is the item's symbol where it has exactly one. The drift fold (E17-5) observes each news and
    filing item once, in knowledge-time order, process-locally at retrieval; an item the detector
    refuses as out of order folds nothing.

### Rationale

Each reading keeps the spec's invariants whole without waiting on the lanes in flight: the
preconditions are enforced by data rather than a flag, the provisional records cannot reach a
production journal, and every reading that the spec leaves open is taken toward refusing more or
saying less. The second entry point keeps the R0 path untouched while giving the shell one place
to hook in after R0.

### Alternatives considered

- **Add `Effect::Research` and handle research in `handle`:** the natural shape, but it forces an
  edit to the shell's exhaustive `Effect` match on the R0 path.
- **Gate runs on a boolean in the configuration:** anyone could set it; a catalogue answer cannot be
  set by a caller in production.
- **Put judging in a layer-6 crate beside the runtime:** the runtime would have to depend on it, and
  two crates at one layer cannot depend on each other.
- **Corroborate on any two cited items:** two items from one source would corroborate themselves.

<a id="dec-1004"></a>

## DEC-1004: CI's schema mutation sweep runs in six shards

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: a reversible engineering decision under DEC-79 that adds parallel shards and removes no check, as DEC-464 directs); item 1's shard count superseded by [DEC-2637](process.md#dec-2637) (ten shards) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The lane I4 builder (Claude Code), under DEC-79 and DEC-464 |
| **Story or claim** | E4-5, E17-8 (their read-model schemas, claim [#1463](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1463)); E21-31's read models ([#1446](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/1446)); supersedes DEC-688 item 2's shard count only |

### Decision

1. The `schema-mutants` matrix runs six shards, `0/6` to `5/6`, `fail-fast: false`, in place of
   DEC-688 item 2's three. Everything else in DEC-688 stands: each shard runs the three checkers on
   the unmutated tree first, the required `full` verdict passes only when every shard succeeded,
   and `cargo xtask check` and the nightly run the sweep unsharded.
2. The xtask test that pins the job's lines pins the six shards.

### Rationale

The sweep was 645 mutants over three shards. The backtest and evaluation read models (DEC-1001,
DEC-1002) add 267 mutants and make each checker pass about twice as slow, because every mutant
validates every example: about 1.8 seconds a mutant locally, so three shards would hold about 300
mutants each, roughly nine minutes before setup, over DEC-464's ten-minute bound once the A1 read
models land too. Six shards hold about 150 mutants, under five minutes each, with room for the
next schemas.

### Alternatives considered

- **Fewer examples.** Rejected: every example kills a mutant, and DEC-464 forbids removing tests
  or safety gates to meet the bound.
- **A larger timeout.** Rejected: DEC-464's bound is per job.

<a id="dec-1011"></a>

## DEC-1011: The scheduler process measures its clock over SNTP itself, paces ticks by its monotonic clock, and runs on paper only

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering decisions under DEC-79 and DEC-176; none loosens a rule, and item 5 adds a refusal) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of claim [#1490](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1490), lane P2 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967, DEC-990), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E21-29; claim #1490; journal spec §5.1, §5.4, §9.20 (DEC-1010); infrastructure design §3.1, §3.4, OPS-14 |

### Context

DEC-1010 closes the scheduler stream's records and the rules a writer holds. This decision records
how the `mandate-scheduler` process writes them, where the spec leaves the choice to the writer.

### Decision

1. **A new safety-critical crate, `mandate-scheduler`, at layer 7.** It uses `mandate-journal`,
   `mandate-journal-pg`, `mandate-process` and `mandate-time`, with `tokio` (`rt`, `signal`) its
   only external dependency, which adds no crate to the lockfile. Its `forbidden_internal` list
   keeps it from the runner, the shell, the CLI, the runtime, the executor and every broker
   connector: it holds nothing secret and places nothing.
2. **The time servers are asked over SNTP (RFC 4330) by the crate itself.** It sends one 48-byte
   request per configured server over `std::net::UdpSocket` and waits one second for each reply.
   A reply counts only when all of these hold:
   - it is a server reply (mode 4);
   - its originate time is the request's transmit time;
   - its leap indicator is not "unsynchronized";
   - its times read.

   No time-protocol crate is added. The host clock is read only at the binary's edge and handed
   in, as ES-05 requires. The servers are configuration (`--servers`); with none, every
   measurement is out of tolerance (DEC-1010 item 3), which withholds openings and nothing else.
3. **Ticks are paced by the process's monotonic clock.** The binary steps once per elapsed
   monotonic second. Each tick is DEC-1010 item 7's later of the last tick plus a second and the
   host clock's second. A slow step catches up on the next tick's value rather than writing a
   burst.
4. **The measurement schedule.** The scheduler measures:
   - at its own start, before its first tick's measurement is due;
   - once in the 15 minutes before each trading day's regular session (`ExchangeCalendar`);
   - every five minutes, half §5.4's ten-minute bound, so one lost measurement stays inside the
     executor's 15-minute window (DEC-1010 item 5).
5. **Paper only.** `Scheduler::start` refuses any environment but paper before it takes the
   stream, as `mandate-agentd` does (DEC-968 item 1). The scheduler writes no order, but it writes
   the environment into every record, and a live workspace's scheduler belongs with the live
   decisions (DEC-967 item 15, E21-48).
6. **One writer epoch, and a batch in doubt is retried as the same bytes.**
   - **Start.** The scheduler takes a newer epoch at start, fencing every earlier one, and replays
     the stream. It writes `StreamOpened` only to an empty stream.
   - **Event ids.** They are derived from the epoch, the head and the ordinal in the scheduler's
     own id space (`2`, after the agent's `0` and the account's `1`).
   - **`Unavailable` or `Ambiguous`.** The batch is kept and appended again unchanged before
     anything new, so the journal answers `AlreadyCommitted` rather than writing twice.
   - **`Fenced`.** The scheduler exits `fenced` before it writes anything more.
   - **Any other refusal.** It exits `cycle_failed`, and the restart replays.
7. **Lifecycle and probes come from `mandate-process`.**
   - **Kind.** The scheduler is a `Writer`: ready once it holds the epoch and has replayed to the
     head.
   - **Probes.** The binary publishes the lifecycle's snapshot to the probe socket after every
     step, and its `probe livez|readyz` subcommand is the `exec` probe (DEC-994 item 5).
   - **Drain.** SIGTERM drains: the scheduler writes nothing new, tries a batch in doubt once
     more, then exits `drained`, or `drain_incomplete` if the batch is still in doubt.
8. **A trading date the calendar cannot answer starts no day.** The previous day's limits then
   stay in force rather than being reset on a guess (`AGENTS.md` rule 3).

### Rationale

The scheduler's one duty is a risk clock that never stops and a record of how good the host clock
was. Keeping the time protocol and the clock reads at the edges keeps everything else pure and
testable with an injected clock and a shared in-memory journal. That includes fencing, restarts,
steps back and a batch in doubt, as `mandate-agentd`'s tests do.

### Alternatives considered

- **Read the offset from the host's NTP daemon (chrony or `adjtimex`).** Rejected: it needs a
  daemon in an image built on an empty base, or `unsafe` system calls the workspace forbids.
- **An SNTP or NTP crate.** Rejected: outside DEC-990's pre-approved kinds, and the 48-byte
  exchange is small enough to own and test.
- **Pace ticks by the host wall clock.** Rejected: a host clock that steps back would stall the
  loop itself.
- **Run on any environment.** Deferred to the live decisions with a refusal now.

<a id="dec-1012"></a>

## DEC-1012: The runtime runs as its own process on the journal alone, reads the kill switch first, and waits for the post-R0 hook for production ports

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering decisions under DEC-79 and DEC-176; item 9 tightens the runtime to journal spec §9 rule 12 and loosens nothing) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of lane P3 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967, DEC-990), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E21-30 slice 1; infrastructure design §3.1, §3.4, §3.6; ADR-0001 ES-06, ES-20; DEC-17, DEC-914, DEC-968 |

### Context

E21-30 asks for the agent runtime and the account executor as long-running processes. The R0
rehearsal holds the paper runner, the shell's run path, the CLI's live entry, and the rehearsal
script until it closes, and the production-cycle constructor (DEC-968 item 6) is the post-R0 hook.
The shell's production ports (its `Bridge`) are private to it. This decision records what slice 1
builds without them and where it stops.

### Decision

1. **Two new safety-critical crates, and two moves that change no behaviour.**
   - `mandate-store` (layer 7): the journal port a long-running process writes through (`Store`,
     over `MemoryJournal` and Postgres), and the shared envelope that turns a draft into the bytes
     the journal hashes. It moves out of `mandate-scheduler`, which now uses it.
   - `mandate-daemon` (layer 8, process group `agent_process`): `RuntimeProcess` and its binary.
     Its `forbidden_internal` list keeps it from the shell, the executor, every broker connector,
     the MCP server, the paper runner, the CLI, and the broker simulator: the runtime has no
     broker credential and no route to a broker by construction (`AGENTS.md` rule 12).
   - The control-stream tail moves from `mandate-shell` into `mandate-runtime` as
     `mandate_runtime::tailed`, and the shell's `tailed` delegates to it with its own error, so the
     shell's tests pass unchanged.
2. **Slice 1 is the runtime process; the executor process is slice 2.** The executor's process,
   whose readiness also waits for reconciliation, is where a not-ready process hands only the kill
   switch and risk exits. It needs the executor's public items and its broker fixture, and lands
   as its own change.
3. **The journal is the only channel; a wake hint only shortens a wait.** `Wake::wait` returns
   early on a hint, and every pass reads the journal whether or not one came, so a lost, late or
   repeated hint costs latency and never changes an outcome (DEC-17, ES-20). The Postgres journal
   has no `NOTIFY` today. Adding one is a migration of the journal schema and lands as its own
   change. Until then the binary waits one second between passes (`Sleep`).
4. **Each pass reads the control stream first.** The control stream is read before anything else,
   so the owner's kill switch is never queued behind a tick (ES-06, design §3.6).
   - **The control stream.** It is handed whole each time its head grows. The core's copy fold
     makes a row handed again inert (DEC-914 item 2, EI-3).
   - **The clock stream.** Its latest `ClockAdvanced` is handed on every pass as the core's risk
     clock. The core evaluates each risk-clock second once and takes a tick it has seen as no input.
   - **A clock stream that cannot be read** hands no tick and holds nothing else.
5. **The runtime process has no readiness gate of its own.** It is not ready in two cases:
   - before its `Input::Started` batch commits;
   - while a batch is in doubt.

   In both, the core accepts only that same input, so there is nothing for a gate to hold back.
6. **Production ports wait for the post-R0 hook (DEC-968 item 6, lane P5).** `RuntimeProcess`
   takes a `Wiring`: the core's ports plus `Refs`, the schema version and configuration references
   each draft is journaled with. A draft the wiring has no references for stops the process
   (`cycle_failed`) rather than being written without them.
   - **The binary today.** It runs only `fixture::wiring`, over an in-memory journal: a gate that
     denies every proposal, a plan that proposes nothing, a flatten planner with nothing held, and
     one fixed mandate version.
   - **Without `--fixture`.** The binary refuses with `deployment_ports_not_wired`. [DEC-1013](earlier-900-1999.md#dec-1013)
     item 1 supersedes this sub-item: without `--fixture` it runs on the journal
     `MANDATE_JOURNAL_DSN` names, still on the fixture wiring.
7. **One writer epoch, and a batch in doubt is offered as the same bytes.**
   - **Start.** The process takes a newer epoch on the agent stream, fencing every earlier one,
     folds the stream, and writes `StreamOpened` only to an empty stream. A `StreamOpened` whose
     append is in doubt exits `cycle_failed` before the core starts; the restart replays and finds
     it.
   - **`Unavailable` or `Ambiguous`.** The input is kept with the event time its drafts were first
     written at, and handed again before anything else. The journal then answers
     `AlreadyCommitted` rather than writing twice.
   - **`Fenced`.** The process exits `fenced` before it writes anything more.
   - **Any other refusal.** It exits `cycle_failed`, and the restart replays.
   - **SIGTERM.** It drains: nothing new is handed, a batch in doubt is tried once more, and the
     process exits `drained`, or `drain_incomplete`.
8. **Intents and timers do not leave the runtime process.**
   - **Intents.** They reach the executor only through the journal (design §3.1).
   - **Deadlines.** A deadline is folded state that the next tick acts on.
   - **Owner alerts.** They are returned only after the drafts they name commit, as a fixed key
     and an opaque event id (`AGENTS.md` rule 6). Delivering them is the notifier's.
9. **An owner's kill switch with no bid shown records its bid members as `null`, and records its
   owner.** Before this change, the core wrote `OwnerExitRequested` with empty text for `bid`,
   `bid_size`, `floor` and `user` when the control-stream kill switch carried no bid. The journal
   refuses that under journal spec §9 rule 12, so the process stopped instead of acting on the
   switch. The copy now records `null` for the bid members and the issuing owner as `user`, as rule
   12 requires. This tightens the core to the spec and changes no outcome a correct writer could
   reach.
10. **Paper only.** `RuntimeProcess::start` refuses any environment but paper before it takes the
    stream, as `mandate-agentd` and the scheduler do (DEC-968 item 1, DEC-1011 item 5).

### Rationale

Slice 1 is the part of E21-30 that holds without touching the R0 path:
- the process shell;
- the journal tailing with the kill switch first;
- the fencing and in-doubt retry;
- the lifecycle and probes.

Every outcome is tested on the real in-memory journal, which validates each draft the process
writes. That is how item 9's defect surfaced. Production ports then plug into the `Wiring` without
changing the loop.

### Alternatives considered

- **Host the shell's production cycle from `mandate-daemon`.** Rejected for now: its ports are
  private, and opening them edits the R0 path, which is the post-R0 hook's to do.
- **A readiness gate in the runtime process.** Rejected: item 5's windows are exactly when the core
  takes only one input, so a gate would hold nothing and add a path to get wrong.
- **Tail only the rows after the last one handed.** Rejected: DEC-914 item 2 hands the control
  stream whole, and the copy fold makes that safe.

<a id="dec-1013"></a>

## DEC-1013: The runtime process runs on the workspace journal in the cell, still on the deny-all wiring

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering decisions under DEC-79 and DEC-176; it supersedes one sub-item of DEC-1012 item 6 and loosens nothing: the wiring stays the fixture's deny-all one) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of lane P3 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967, DEC-990), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E21-30 slice 1 follow-up; the E2E lane's gap 5 (`cargo xtask cell demo` starts the runtime); #1521 |

### Context

DEC-1012 item 6 let `mandate-daemon` run only over an in-memory journal, and refuse without
`--fixture`, until the post-R0 hook wires production ports. The cell's `processes.json` listed the
runtime but marked it not built, so `cargo xtask cell demo` never started it, and the chart had no
way to start a service or probe it. This decision runs the same process, on the same deny-all
wiring, on the cell's Postgres journal.

### Decision

1. **The binary runs on the journal `MANDATE_JOURNAL_DSN` names.** Without `--fixture`,
   `mandate-daemon run` opens that journal through `mandate-store`'s `PgStore`, as the scheduler
   does, and with none set it refuses with `runtime_start_failed: journal_dsn_missing` rather than
   running on a journal of its own. `--fixture` keeps the in-memory journal. This supersedes DEC-1012
   item 6's "Without `--fixture`" sub-item only.
2. **The wiring stays deny-all in both.** Both journals run `fixture::wiring`: a gate that denies
   every proposal, a plan that proposes nothing, and a flatten planner with nothing held. The
   process can journal its own stream's opening and the owner's kill switch, and nothing else. No
   order can be proposed, and the crate still reaches no broker (`xtask/layers.toml`). The production
   ports remain the post-R0 hook (DEC-968 item 6, lane P5). Paper only, as before.
3. **The cell builds and runs it.** `cargo xtask cell up` builds `mandate-daemon` statically
   beside the CLI, as the `runtime` image (`deploy/cell/images/runtime.Dockerfile`: scratch,
   non-root, the binary only), tagged by the binary's SHA-256, imports it, and sets
   `images.runtime`. `processes.json` marks `runtime` built. Where the `mandate` image is pulled by
   digest instead, no runtime image is published yet, so the runtime is left out of that cell and
   the demo names it skipped.
4. **A service's launch lives in the process list.** A service in `processes.json` may carry
   `args`, which start it, and `probe`, its own binary asked `livez` or `readyz` over its socket on
   the pod's `/tmp` (DEC-994 item 5). `{workspace}` and the new `runtime` values (`agent`,
   `connection`, `owner`: ids only) fill them. The chart renders them as the container's arguments
   and its `exec` readiness and liveness probes, and Compose as its command and `CMD` health check.
   A runtime image named without its deployment fails the render rather than starting a runtime for
   no agent (`AGENTS.md` rule 3).
5. **The local cell runs one demo deployment.** `deploy/cell/values/k3d.yaml` names agent
   `demo-agent`, connection `demo-paper` and owner `demo-owner` in workspace `ws1`. `cargo xtask
   cell demo` reports the runtime ready; the paper agent part still waits on the executor, and so
   does research's model gateway line: a deny-all runtime runs no research loop, so that line waits
   for the post-R0 wiring rather than reading as ready.
6. **A stopping journal waits 10 seconds for its clients.** The journal chart's default for
   CloudNativePG's `smartShutdownTimeout` is 10 seconds (a value, at most 180), so it holds for every
   cell the chart renders, the managed one included, not only k3d. With the runtime in the cell, the
   restart drill went from 33 seconds to three and a half minutes, which fits the default 180-second
   smart shutdown waiting on the runtime's pooled connection before the fast shutdown that follows
   it anyway; that is the observed cause, not one a run has isolated. A fast shutdown aborts only
   uncommitted transactions; an acknowledged commit is durable (`synchronous_commit`, `fsync`).

### Rationale

The E2E lane needs the runtime running in the cell, and the cell's journal is the one the
runtime will run on after R0. Running the deny-all wiring there exercises the process's real
lifecycle: its epoch on Postgres, its probes, its drain on SIGTERM, and the chart's network policy,
without any route to an order.

### Alternatives considered

- **Keep `--fixture` in the cell.** The process would run, but on a journal of its own inside the
  pod, so nothing in the cell could see it, and the Postgres path would first run with the post-R0
  hook.
- **Wire production ports now.** That is the post-R0 hook's, and it touches R0's files.
- **Pass the deployment by environment variables.** The binary already takes flags. Filling the
  flags from values keeps one way to start it, in the cell and by hand.

<a id="dec-1014"></a>

## DEC-1014: The runtime process folds the account stream its connection's establishment names

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: a reversible engineering decision under DEC-79 and DEC-176; it lets the runtime process take the account stream's facts the shell already folds, every reading it takes is the stricter one, and item 6 tightens the core's startup hold) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of lane P3 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967, DEC-990), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E21-30 slice 1; trading spec §11 (the startup hold); journal spec §2, §9.8; DEC-131 items 13 and 22; DEC-914 item 2; DEC-1012 |

### Context

`mandate-daemon`'s runtime process (DEC-1012) read the control and clock streams and never the
account stream. The core's startup hold lifts only on a clean `ReconciliationRun` folded from the
account stream (DEC-131 item 13), so its effective mode stayed `paused` for good: no research run
could start and no proposal could be made. The executor's `AgentModeApplied`, and every other
account-stream fact the shell's copy fold takes, never reached it either.

The account stream is `acct:{workspace}:{account_ref}`, and `account_ref` is not the connection id:
an account stream belongs to the connection whose version-2 `ConnectionEstablished` on the control
stream names its `account_ref` (journal spec §9.8).

### Decision

1. **Each pass folds the account stream after the control stream and before the clock stream.**
   The rows past the core's head for that stream are folded with `mandate_runtime::fold`, as the
   shell folds them, so a row is folded once and the core's own sequence check refuses a gap. The
   next input the core takes (each pass's tick) settles the startup hold and journals the mode
   change. The owner's kill switch is still read first (ES-06).
2. **The start folds it too, before `Input::Started`,** as the shell's open replays the account
   stream before the start, so the hold is taken or not on the whole fold.
3. **The account is the one the latest version-2 `ConnectionEstablished` of the deployment's
   connection, in the process's environment, names.** Another connection's establishment, another
   environment's, and a version-1 record name nothing. Until one names it, the process follows no
   account, and the hold stays. A later `ConnectionRevoked` is not read: a revoked connection's
   executor is torn down and writes nothing more, and what its account stream already holds can
   only tighten the runtime or lift its hold on a reconciliation the executor did run.
4. **It fails closed.**
   - A start that cannot read the control stream, or the account stream it names, is never ready.
   - A pass that cannot read the account stream folds nothing from it and still hands the control
     stream and the tick, so neither the owner's kill switch (ES-06) nor a risk exit or deadline
     waits on it (`AGENTS.md` rule 13). An opening decided on a mode that may be stale still meets
     the executor's gate, which applies the account's restrictions itself (rule 1).
     The executor process (E21-30 slice 2) therefore has this as an acceptance criterion: its gate
     refuses an opening intent for an agent whose mode it holds as `paused` or `exits_only`,
     whatever the runtime proposed.
   - A record the core cannot fold, or an account row journaled for another environment, stops
     the process (DEC-85, `AGENTS.md` rule 8), as an unfoldable control or agent row already does.
   - A running process never changes the account it follows. A later establishment that names
     another account stops it, and the next process follows the new account from its start.
5. **The runtime only reads the account stream.** It never takes ownership of it or writes it. The
   account's executor stays its single writer (journal spec §2).
6. **A startup hold waits for a reconciliation after the start.** When `Input::Started` takes the
   hold, the core forgets the reconciliation its replay folded, so only a clean `ReconciliationRun`
   folded after that start lifts it. The hold is taken exactly when that earlier reconciliation was
   judged not enough (an intent outstanding, or a submission after it), so it cannot be what lifts
   it. Without this, `settle_startup_hold`'s looser test (no "nothing outstanding") would lift the
   hold on the next input after a restart with an intent still outstanding. The shell never hit
   this, because it runs a fresh reconciliation right after `Started`, and its outcomes are
   unchanged. This tightens `mandate-runtime` and loosens nothing.
   A runtime process restarted while held therefore waits for the executor's next clean
   reconciliation, so the executor process (E21-30 slice 2) must reconcile periodically or when a
   runtime restarts. That is one of its acceptance criteria, with a test of a copied
   `ConnectionEstablished` on the account stream (item 7).
7. **The executor's connection records still stop the process.** The account stream will carry
   the executor's `ConnectionStateChanged` and `ConnectionCredentialRefreshed` and its copies of
   `ConnectionEstablished` and `ConnectionCredentialRotated` (journal spec §9.8). The core still
   refuses them `not_interpreted`, as DEC-969 item 5 keeps them until their writer exists, so a
   runtime that meets one fails closed. The executor process (E21-30 slice 2), their first writer,
   decides how the runtime folds them. Connections spec §9 already says a connection's state
   reaches the mode machine only through the executor's `AccountRestrictionChanged` and
   `AgentModeApplied`.

### Rationale

The shell's in-process run already folds the account stream beside the agent stream. The runtime
process is the same core on the journal alone (DEC-1012), so it takes the same facts the same way.
What the shell got from its sequencing, a reconciliation run after every start, the runtime
process cannot: its executor reconciles on its own schedule. Item 6 puts that ordering into the
core instead, so neither process can lift a hold on knowledge from before the start.

### Alternatives considered

- **A `--account` flag.** One more thing to keep in step with the journal, and a wrong value would
  let another account's reconciliation lift this agent's hold.
- **Hand the account rows as `Input::Journal`, as the control rows are.** The core folds account
  facts rather than taking them as input, and refuses a row it has already folded, so handing the
  stream whole each pass would stop the process.
- **Follow a new account without a restart.** The core keeps one reconciliation state, so an old
  account's reconciliation could lift the hold for the new one.
- **Fold the executor's connection records as inert here.** That supersedes DEC-969 item 5 from a
  lane that does not write them; their writer's lane decides it (item 7).

### Consequences

- In the local cell nothing changes yet: the cell's demo deployment's connection is never
  established on the control stream, so the runtime follows no account and stays held.
- Research (lane I2) and trading can run in the process once the executor reconciles after the
  runtime's start, and once item 7's records are folded or absent.

<a id="dec-1015"></a>

## DEC-1015: A runtime process started by the deployment manager takes its deployment from the pod's variables

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: a reversible engineering decision under DEC-79 and DEC-176, agreed with lane D1; it adds a way to name a deployment and loosens nothing) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of lane P3 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967, DEC-990), with lane D1 (E21-33) |
| **Story or claim** | E21-30 slice 1, E21-33 slice 2; DEC-1012, DEC-1013 |

### Context

The deployment manager (`mandate-deploy-manager`, E21-33) starts one pod per runtime from a chart
