5. **One working opening per instrument.** While an opening or increase the runtime proposed in an
   instrument still works (item 4), another opening or increase there is denied in the runtime's
   dry run as `working_order_limit`, trading spec §5.3 rule 6's code: "At most one working
   non-protective order per instrument per account". This happens before the gate is asked,
   journaled as the `DecisionMade` deny, and the same holds at a grant's re-validation (mandate
   spec §6.4 check 11). The R0 run's second ask would now be held. The binding gate keeps its own
   checks. An exit, a protective order and a kill switch are never read against it (`AGENTS.md`
   rule 13). Mandate spec §8.3 step 3, the size net of working openings, is the builder's and is
   not changed here.
6. **Two tests move with item 5.**
   - The fail-closed suite's `--new-cycle` test fills its first order before starting the second
     cycle. Without the fill, rule 6 holds the second opening, which is the reading this decision
     takes.
   - The rh-sim restart test's opening behind an `Unknown` order is now held by the runtime's dry
     run, before the gate, and the test asserts exactly that: a `DecisionMade` denied
     `working_order_limit` and no new `GateDecided`. The opening was blocked before as well, by
     the binding gate's `unknown_order_in_flight`.

7. **The connector names a listed record by the broker id it holds.** A broker that does not
   echo our `ref_id` lists our order with no client order id. That is the rehearsal's simulator,
   and DEC-941 item 4 allows it for Robinhood. Items 2 and 3 would then adopt nothing, and the
   watch would never see its fill. The Robinhood connector already holds, for each key it
   placed, the broker's order id the place answered (it cancels by it, DEC-860 item 7, and
   restores it from the journal). So a listed record carrying no `ref_id` whose order id is that
   one names our key. A record with a foreign `ref_id` still names nothing of ours. A connector
   that never placed the key, and holds no id for it, names nothing. The broker id is read by the
   connector, as journal spec §9.16 allows, never by the executor's fold.
8. **What the rehearsal showed next (R19).** With items 1 to 7, the watch adopts the entry as
   `filled` and the second ask is held. But no `FillApplied` is journaled: the executor books a
   fill only from a fill report, and Robinhood's path has none. Reconciliation then finds the
   broker's 1 share against an empty ledger and pauses the agent, which is the safe stop. B3's
   protective stop is never sized. That is recorded as R19 and not decided here.

### Rationale

The watch is how a placing run learns of its fill. On a profile that cannot query by id, the only
honest read is the listing the profile offers, matched by our own id. A record matched by guess
could book a fill that never happened. Following the opening through the journal lets the runtime
apply rule 6 the same way live and on replay, without trusting the run context's
`working_opening_cost` of zero.

### Alternatives considered

- **Store the broker's order id in the executor's fold, and match it**: journal spec §9.16 rules
  this out; the fold does not read it.
- **Hold while any intent is outstanding**: the runtime never retires an outstanding intent, so
  this would hold every opening after the first, forever.
- **Fix the run context's `working_opening_cost` instead**: that is the builder's sizing (§8.3), a
  larger change, and it would not stop an ask the builder still proposes.

<a id="dec-1151"></a>

## DEC-1151: A fill Robinhood never reports is booked from its order record and the position; the stop goes out beside the one opening (R0 R19 to R21)

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering readings under DEC-79, each under DEC-176. Items 1 to 4 fill a gap, how a fill is known on a broker with no fill report, by the strictest reading: a fill is booked only where the broker's own record and position leave one reading, so none is ever invented. Item 5 brings the tracer to `AGENTS.md` rule 13 and to DEC-940 item 2's own wording. Item 6 extends DEC-1150 item 7 to the snapshot. Item 7 changes the simulator only. Item 8 sets the rehearsal mandate to what V-008 asks. None touches live money, spending, legal text or a safety rule) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The R19 builder (Claude Code), under DEC-79 and DEC-176; one PR with its tests first (DEC-921) |
| **Story or claim** | E7-26, E7-23; the [R0 rehearsal](../tasks/r0-rehearsal.md)'s R19, and R20 to R22 found behind it; claim #1428 |

On the Tuesday rehearsal clock, after R18's fix, the watch adopted the entry `filled` from its
order record. No fill was ever journaled, because the executor books a fill only from a fill
report, and Robinhood publishes none (connections spec U-R8: no activity feed, fill fields
unpublished). The reconciliation then read the broker's share against an empty ledger,
`BrokerPositionObserved` `mismatch`, and paused the agent (R19). Behind R19, the rehearsal met
three more stops, each recorded below: the tracer refused to send B3's protective stop (R20),
the reconciliation read that stop as the broker's own order (R21), and the rehearsal mandate set
no stop-limit offset (R22).

### Decision

1. **The broker's word on a fill, where it publishes no fill.** Robinhood's order record carries
   `filled_quantity` and no price; its position carries `average_buy_price`. U-R8 names the
   comparison DEC-529's order runs on a flat, dedicated account: the order's state and filled
   quantity, and the position. So the fill is read from the two, and only where they leave one
   reading (`mandate_executor::recorded_fills`):
   - the order is ours, a buy whose purpose adds risk, sent `day` in the regular session, and its
     broker record reported a filled quantity above zero and no more than its own quantity, none
     of it booked;
   - the ledger holds the instrument flat, with no unattributed fill in it, and every other
     order in the instrument has booked all its record reported, so no other fill can be inside
     the broker's average;
   - the broker lists exactly one position in the instrument, long exactly the reported quantity,
     at an average no higher than the order's limit. No fill of a buy limit can be above its
     limit, so an average above it describes something else, such as the owner's own trades in
     the account.

   The fill is that quantity at that average price. Its trade date is the New York date of the
   order's `OrderSubmitted`, since a `day` order fills only in the session it was sent in.
2. **The idempotency key.** The fill's id is `record:<client order id>:<filled quantity>`. Every
   read of the same record derives the same id, so the journal books it once (journal spec
   §5.2), and a second derivation from the booked state derives nothing, because the order's
   booked quantity is no longer zero.
3. **Where it is booked.** Robinhood's reconciler hands these fills to the executor's
   reconciliation in its snapshot (`ExecutorReconciler::without_activities`). Step 2 books them
   through the executor's own fill path before step 3 compares positions (trading spec §11). An
   order already terminal books `LateFillApplied`, and one still working books `FillApplied` and
   its state (§5.7). So the reconciliation that follows the adoption finds the position explained,
   and the next start's own-fill pass (DEC-955) finds nothing owed.
4. **Never invented.** With no record of a fill, a ledger that is not flat, a second order with an
   unbooked fill in the instrument, a short position, two positions, or a position that is not
   exactly the reported quantity, nothing is derived. The position stays unexplained and the
   reconciliation pauses the agent, as before (`AGENTS.md` rule 3). Fees: the fill carries no
   broker fee, since Robinhood charges no commission (DEC-958 item 1). Its regulatory fees are the
   fee configuration's, which every fill record names (`config_refs`, DEC-958 and DEC-960).
5. **R20: the protective stop goes out beside the one opening.** The tracer sends one order per
   run (FT-1), and counted every submission. On a profile with no bracket, B3's stop-limit is a
   second submission after the fill, so the run stopped with `the tracer places one order per run,
   and the executor asked for another` before the stop reached the broker. DEC-940 item 2 already
   reads "An exit, a protective order or a flatten always passes", and `AGENTS.md` rule 13 exempts
   them from every such limit. The rule now counts only a submission whose purpose adds risk: a
   second opening is still refused (`one_order_only`), and a protective order or an exit always
   goes out. The watch watches the run's one opening, never a protective order sent before it.
   A run that sends only an exit or a protective order is not watched: the watch exists for the
   entry's fill (DEC-858). Protective orders and exits are then not counted per run here at all;
   the executor's gate and protection sequencing bound them, as on every other path.
6. **R21: the snapshot names our working orders.** A Robinhood record names our order only by
   `ref_id`, which the broker may not echo (U-R2, DEC-941 item 4). The account snapshot named
   nothing ours, so the reconciliation read the resting stop as an order of the broker's own. It
   drafted `ExternalActivityIngested`, which the journal refuses (DEC-956 item 5). The snapshot
   now names a record exactly as DEC-1150 item 7 names a listed one. It is our key when the record
   echoes that key's derived `ref_id`, or, echoing none, when its order id is the one this
   connector's place answered or its journal restored. A record with another `ref_id`, or with
   none and an id this connector never placed, names nothing. The executor's fold still reads no
   broker id (journal spec §9.16), so DEC-956 item 2's proposal stays the founder's.
7. **The simulator's cash.** `mandate-rh-sim` held cash and buying power fixed whatever filled.
   After item 3 booked the fill, the ledger's cash was the cost of the share below the broker's,
   so the reconciliation would have mismatched on cash. Real Robinhood spends a buy's cost from
   cash. The simulator now spends a buy's fill cost from both figures, and adds a sell's proceeds
   to neither. Under DEC-902 item 2, `get_portfolio`'s `cash` is settled cash, and adding unsettled
   proceeds would lift buying power. That is the reading that adds no risk, the simulator's own
   rule where the contract is silent. The simulator does not refuse a fill beyond cash: buying
   power, which `review` and `place` already check, is what refuses an order, and a fill below
   zero cash is a test's own choice. The rehearsal never reaches a sell, so it cannot show how
   the platform's cash model meets Robinhood's after a sale (below).
8. **R22: the rehearsal mandate sets the stop-limit offset.** Robinhood protects US equities with a
   stop-limit only. With `stop_limit_offset` unset (version 1: `crypto_stop_limit_offset`), B3
   had no form to place and journaled `expiry_unreplaceable`. Mandate spec V-008 requires the
   offset where the profile protects with a stop-limit ("US equities on Robinhood"), and without
   the profile it counts every asset class as protected by one. The rehearsal mandate now sets
   `0.01`.

### Not closed here

- **V-008's code is looser than the spec (R22).** `mandate-spec`'s `document_rules` requires the
  offset only for crypto, so `version create` and `version confirm` accepted the rehearsal
  mandate. The spec's reading only refuses more. It is a `mandate-spec` change with reference
  cases, for its own PR. Until it lands, the founder's live mandate must set
  `crypto_stop_limit_offset`, or the position is unprotected and the run says so
  (`expiry_unreplaceable`, `protection_expiring`).
- **A sale's cash on Robinhood.** The platform's model credits a sell's proceeds at once. If
  Robinhood's `cash` adds them only at settlement, the first reconciliation after a live sell is
  out of the cash band. That alerts at once and pauses after DEC-146's second run. R0's order is a
  buy, and its stop's fill would be the first sell. The founder's check in the app after a sale
  answers it.
- **A second fill of a part-booked entry.** Once a part of an entry is booked from its record,
  item 1 derives nothing more for that entry: its booked quantity is no longer zero. If the rest
  then fills, the position is unexplained and the agent pauses, fail safe. The next start's
  own-fill pass then asks the broker by client order id, as in the next item. R0's order is one
  share, which fills whole.
- **A restart before the fill is booked.** If a run ends after the adoption and before a
  reconciliation books the fill, the next start's own-fill pass asks the broker by client order
  id. Robinhood does not offer that request, so the start stops, fail closed: `the broker's
  answer for an owned order`.
- **Spec text.** Items 1 to 4 belong beside U-R8 and trading spec §11, and item 5 beside FT-1.
  ES-22 keeps spec text out of a code change, so these follow as a spec PR.

### Why this is DEC-176's to take, and adds no risk

DEC-176 lets an agent take a reading that refuses more, or that fills a gap by the reading that
adds no risk; anything that weakens a safety rule is the founder's (DEC-79). This change was
asked for by the R0 coordinator's dispatch for R19 ("journal the fill … from the broker's order
record … A fill must never be invented: no broker record means no fill, and the agent stays
paused"). It is taken here as a gap filled, for three reasons:

- **The share is held either way.** The pause did not remove the exposure. It left a real share
  outside the ledger, so the gate's limits, the drawdown ladder and B3's protection all read a
  flat account while the broker held a position. Booking it makes every limit count what the
  account holds. That is the side that adds no risk.
- **Nothing is believed that a fill report would not say.** A fill report carries quantity,
  price, side and order. Here the quantity is the broker's own `filled_quantity` for our order,
  the side and the order are ours, and the price is the broker's own average for a position that
  item 1's conditions make that order's alone. Where those figures leave a second reading, nothing
  is booked.
- **The safety stop still stands for everything it caught before.** Trading spec §11 still
  compares positions after step 2, and a position the derived fill does not explain still
  pauses. The only position no longer paused on is one the broker's own record and position
  explain exactly.

Should the founder read this as weakening §11's stop instead, reverting items 1 to 4 restores the
pause, and nothing else in the change depends on them.

### Rationale

The broker holds the share, and the journal must say why before anything else reads the
account. On a flat, dedicated account, the position's average buy price is the fill's price, with
no other fill to blend in. That is the comparison U-R8 already names, so nothing beyond the
broker's own figures is believed. Every case with more than one reading derives nothing, and the
reconciliation then pauses the agent. Items 5 and 6 let the protection B3 places reach the broker
and be recognized there. Without them, a filled share would rest unprotected or stop the run.

### Alternatives considered

- **Book the fill at adoption, in the listing's path.** The listing's record carries no price, and
  the executor's step has no position to read. A price from the order's limit would be invented.
- **Price the fill at the order's limit.** It is a bound, not a price, and would misstate cash and
  P&L.
- **Leave R19 to a broker read of executions.** The contract offers none (U-R8). The founder's
  live order would then always pause the agent after its fill.
- **Count the protective stop as the one order, and send the entry in a second run.** That leaves
  a filled share unprotected across runs, which rule 13 forbids.

<a id="dec-1152"></a>

## DEC-1152: V-008 requires the stop-limit offset wherever the connection's profile protects with a stop-limit; the rehearsal scripts the stop's trigger (R0 R22, R23)

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering readings under DEC-79, each under DEC-176. Items 1 and 2 build DEC-539 item 2, which the founder accepted, and only refuse more: a paper mandate reads Alpaca's row and is judged exactly as before, and a live mandate is refused V-008 where it allows an equity and sets no offset. None V-008 refused before is admitted. The founder may revert items 1 and 2, which restores the crypto-only check. Item 3 changes the simulator only. Item 4 records what the rehearsal showed and decides nothing. None touches live money, spending, legal text or a safety rule) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The R0 builder (Claude Code), under DEC-79 and DEC-176; one PR with its tests first (DEC-921) |
| **Story or claim** | E7-26, E10-1; DEC-539 item 2's follow-up; the [R0 rehearsal](../tasks/r0-rehearsal.md)'s R22 and R23; claim #1428 |

DEC-1151 found that `mandate-spec` asked for the stop-limit offset only for crypto, so a live
Robinhood equity mandate with no offset was confirmed and deployed, and B3 then had no form to
protect its share with (R22). Mandate spec V-008 and DEC-539 item 2 already say what V-008 reads:
the classes the connection's profile protects with a stop-limit, failing closed without the
profile. This change builds that, and scripts the rehearsal's market move through the stop.

### Decision

1. **V-008 and W-002 read the connection's profile.**
   - `ValidationContext` gains `stop_limit_asset_classes`: the classes the connection's
     capability profile protects with a stop-limit, or `None` for the profile absent.
   - V-008 requires the offset, with protection enabled, when any class the universe allows is in
     it. With the profile absent, any allowed class counts, so V-008 fails closed (mandate spec
     §4).
   - W-002's worst case, and `worst_case_stop_distance`, add the offset under the same test
     (DEC-539 item 3).
   - Disabled protection with an offset is still V-008, whatever the profile.
   - Version 1's `crypto_stop_limit_offset` is the offset (DEC-539 item 5). The schema's version 2
     rename is not built here.
2. **Where the classes come from.**
   - **The control-stream fold.** `ValidationContext::from_journal` reads them from the
     connection's latest `ConnectionEstablished`, which now carries its `broker`, by DEC-539 item
     2's fixed table (`mandate_spec::context::stop_limit_asset_classes`):
     - `alpaca`: crypto only, since equities get OCO or bracket legs;
     - `robinhood`: equities and crypto, since equities get a stop-limit only (DEC-529 item 7);
     - any other broker, a record naming none, a revoked connection, or none at all: the profile
       absent.
   - **A connection the stream does not record.** Phase 1's paper connections journal no
     `ConnectionEstablished` and are Alpaca's (DEC-969 item 6, LT-14), so a paper mandate on one
     reads Alpaca's row. A live mandate reads the profile absent: a live connection cannot be
     recorded without its record. This is `unrecorded_stop_limit_asset_classes`, read by the CLI's
     confirm and deploy (`check_rules`) and by the run's control-stream check
     (`confirmed_version`).
   - **The run's own validation context** (`run_context`). A paper connection reads Alpaca's row.
     A live one reads the profile absent, which for v1's two classes is Robinhood's row.
   - **The refcases harness** reads `stop_limit_asset_classes` from the cases' context and from
     `validation_context_defaults` (`[crypto]`). MC-K08, the version-1 case, now passes; the other
     MC-K cases need schema version 2 and fail as before.
   - Consequence for the founder: a live Robinhood mandate with no `crypto_stop_limit_offset` is
     refused `version_invalid` at `version confirm`, and the rehearsal's mandate sets `0.01`.
3. **The rehearsal's market.** The rehearsal simulator answers `POST /rehearsal/market` with
   exactly `{"quotes": {symbol: decimal text}}`:
   - it moves each quote, which triggers every stop the quote reaches;
   - it offers the script's unused fills to every working order, as a place offers them to the
     order it creates;
   - it answers every order's record.

   Only a rehearsal server, which listens on loopback alone, has the route. Any other body is
   `400` with nothing changed. Step 2t uses it: the market falls to 80.5, through B3's stop of
   80.76, and the script's sell fill takes the stop at 80.5, inside its limit of 79.9524. The
   account is flat at the simulator.
4. **What the rehearsal showed next (R23, not decided here).** Step 2u, the same command on the
   account the stop left flat, is refused before any place call, but by the wrong check: `the
   account the connection binds is not flat`. Two things combine:
   - the simulator lists the sold-out position as a row of quantity zero, which DEC-902 item 3
     allows;
   - the lapse counts position rows, not their quantity (`crates/mandate-paper/src/connection.rs`,
     `lapse`).

   Refusing is the safe stop either way. The designed answer is `the connection record is spent`:
   the entry's fill spends the record (DEC-910 item 3), and DEC-529 item 13 allows one order per
   live record. Counting a zero row as flat refuses less, so it is the founder's to accept, with
   its tests first.

### Not closed here

- **The owner's flatten on a second entry.** Each of these needs its own decision or story, so
  the rehearsal does not script them:
  - DEC-529 item 13 and DEC-910 item 3 refuse a second entry on the same live record by design;
    a second entry needs a fresh connection record, which means a new connect check, record,
    version and deployment.
  - The shipping CLI's `mandate agent` command offers `deploy` alone. The owner's exit, pause,
    stop and kill switch exist in `mandate-cli`'s library (`agent.rs`), but no command reaches
    them, so the owner's flatten is not reachable from the CLI.
  - On Robinhood a sell's fill has no published price, the order record nor the position: the
    position's average is the buys'. DEC-1151's `recorded_fills` books buys only. The stop's fill
    is never booked, and the next run's own-fill pass asks the broker by client order id for the
    owned stop, which Robinhood does not offer, so it would stop fail closed (not reached: the
    lapse refuses first).
- **Spec text.** The table in item 2 is DEC-539 item 2's. MC-K08's status follows in a spec PR,
  since `status.toml` is protected (ES-22).

### Rationale

The spec already said what V-008 reads, and DEC-539 gave the table to read until the profile is
an input. Building it refuses exactly the mandates that would deploy unprotected on Robinhood.
Every paper mandate stays as it was, because a paper connection is Alpaca's. The market route
lets the rehearsal show the stop working at the broker. The runs after it show what a sold-out
position meets, without inventing a price for a sale nobody reported.

### Alternatives considered

- **Pass the capability profile itself into validation.** It is cleaner, but `mandate-spec` would
  then read `mandate-domain`'s profile cells. DEC-539 named the fixed table as the step before it.
- **Treat a connection the stream does not record as the profile absent, paper included.** That
  is strictest, but every Phase 1 paper deployment of an equity mandate would then be refused
  V-008, though its connection is Alpaca's by construction.
- **Count a zero position row as flat in the lapse.** It is not decided here, because it refuses
  less (item 4).

<a id="dec-1172"></a>

## DEC-1172: The deployment manager runs as its own process in the cell, with the least access to the Kubernetes API its job needs

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering decisions under DEC-79 and DEC-176; each narrows a pod to its job, and none loosens a rule. The coordinator agreed items 4 to 6 before they were built, as not reserved for the founder) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (builder of claim [#1491](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1491), lane D1 of the managed-cells plan, DEC-967, DEC-990), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E21-33 slice 2; claim #1491; infrastructure design §3.1, §3.4, §9; [DEC-1170](DEC-1170.md) item 9; [DEC-1171](DEC-1171.md) |

### Context

[DEC-1170](DEC-1170.md) built the deployment manager as a library, `mandate-deploy-manager`, with a
fake orchestrator and a Kubernetes adapter, and left its process to this slice (item 9): the loop,
its probes, the Postgres journal port, the chart's templates and RBAC. The cell's demo (E21-54,
DEC-2002) names "the agent's processes started from `AgentDeployed`" as a gap this slice closes.

Three things in the cell stood in the way:
- The manager's crate sits at layer 4, below the Postgres journal (layer 6).
- Every pod's first container, the egress gate, refuses to start until the Kubernetes API server
  stops answering, because that is how it proves its own network policy is programmed. The
  manager has to reach that server to create pods.
- No pod has a service account token today.

### Decision

1. **A process crate, `mandate-deployd`, safety-critical, at layer 8, beside the scheduler and the
   runtime's daemon.** It wires the manager's ports and decides nothing:
   - the control stream over `mandate-journal-pg`;
   - the Kubernetes adapter, built from the pod's mounted service-account files, never from an
     environment variable (DEC-822 item 4);
   - operator alerts as one stderr line each, a code and a runbook only;
   - `mandate-process`'s lifecycle and exec probes over a Unix socket, the same as the scheduler.

   It reads the host clock only at its edge (ES-05). It passes once a second and drains on
   SIGTERM. Its `forbidden_internal` list is the manager's: no crate that writes an agent or
   account stream, holds a broker credential, or edits a mandate.

2. **Each pass's outcome folds into the lifecycle the fail-closed way.**
   - A pass that could not read, write or list, or that found the stream not yet opened, is a
     heartbeat; the next pass starts again from the journal, having done nothing unjournaled.
   - A record the journal refuses as invalid is a defect: the process exits rather than retry it
     blind.

3. **The journal port takes a writer epoch for each append and writes at the head it reads then**,
   because workspace control services share the control stream (journal spec §5.1). It is the same
   protocol the API server uses. A record's derived `event_id` makes a retry, or a second manager's
   draft of the same step, one event.

4. **The manager's service account reaches pods only, and an admission policy bounds the pods it
   can make.**
   - **The Role is pods only.** A namespaced Role's only rule is `pods` in the core group, with
     `create`, `get`, `list` and `delete`. It has no secrets, no `exec`, no other resource or verb,
     and no ClusterRole or cluster scope. A RoleBinding binds it to the manager's ServiceAccount
     alone. Only the manager's pod mounts a token; every other pod keeps
     `automountServiceAccountToken: false`.
   - **Creating pods is itself powerful.** RBAC cannot shape a pod, and the namespace has no Pod
     Security label. Unbounded, `create pods` would let a compromised manager mount any Secret in
     the namespace (another role's `journal-role-*` pgpass), name any ServiceAccount, claim any
     process's egress labels, or run a privileged pod or one with a host path.
   - **A ValidatingAdmissionPolicy bounds it to pods shaped like the chart's templates.** It is
     `admissionregistration.k8s.io/v1`, GA since Kubernetes 1.30; the cell runs k3s v1.35.5. The
     chart renders it and its binding only with the manager, named with the namespace and release
     as a prefix, since both are cluster-scoped. It matches the creation and update of core pods in
     the release's namespace, and, by a match condition, only requests whose user is
     `system:serviceaccount:<namespace>:deploy-manager`, so no other client or pod is affected. It
     fails closed (`failurePolicy: Fail`, `validationActions: [Deny]`). A pod the manager makes
     must:
     - mount no service-account token (`automountServiceAccountToken: false`) and name no service
       account but `default`;
     - share no host network, PID or IPC namespace, and mount no host path;
     - have every container and init container unprivileged with `allowPrivilegeEscalation:
       false`, and no ephemeral container;
     - run only the images its rendered templates name (the runtime's, the executor's, and the
       gate's and credentials' `images.postgres`);
     - mount only memory (`emptyDir`) and the Secrets its rendered templates name: the started
       processes' own role Secrets and `journal-ca`; read no Secret through `env`, `envFrom` or an
       image pull secret;
     - carry `app.kubernetes.io/managed-by: mandate-deploy-manager`, a `mandate.dev/process` that
       has a template, and the release's `mandate.dev/workspace`;
     - carry exactly that process's `mandate.dev/egress-*` labels from processes.json, so a runtime
       cannot claim the executor's vault or broker egress.

     The images, Secrets and egress labels are list and map literals built from the rendered
     templates and processes.json, never by hand.
   - **Residual: what the policy does not check.** A pod inside these bounds may still set any
     other field: its command and arguments, its resources, its node placement (`nodeName`,
     affinity, tolerations), its pod-level `runAsUser`, its capabilities, its
     `readOnlyRootFilesystem`, and any label outside those above, such as the subject or
     generation. It runs one of the templates' images with one of their roles, so it can
     do what a runtime or executor can, as that process. The manager can also delete any pod in its
     namespace, the journal's Postgres pods included, which their operator restarts.

5. **One new egress target, `kube-api`, for the manager's pod alone.**
   - A NetworkPolicy cannot select the API server by pod label (in k3s it is not a pod), so it is
     an external-style target: its CIDRs and port come from values, and an empty list allows
     nothing.
   - `cargo xtask cell up` reads the API server's addresses from the cluster's `kubernetes`
     EndpointSlice and passes each as a /32 with its port (6443 in k3s, behind the Service's 443).
     No value file can hold them, because docker picks the k3d network when the cluster is made.
   - No other process lists it, and the chart's invariant tests check that.

6. **The manager's egress gate proves its policy by probing a destination that policy denies**,
   instead of the API server its policy allows: the cluster resolver's metrics port, 9153, at the
   `nameserver` of the pod's `/etc/resolv.conf`. Every policy allows the resolver on port 53 only.
   Every other pod's gate is unchanged. The proof keeps its meaning, since a pod whose filter is not
   yet programmed reaches everything, the denied port included.
   - The vault and the journal were rejected as probe targets. The vault has no pod in the local
     cell, so it never answers, filtered or not. The journal's Services expose only the port the
     manager is allowed.
   - Residual: on a cluster whose resolver serves no metrics port, the gate reads as filtered at
     once.

7. **The runtime's and executor's pods are the chart's templates, which the manager reads.** The
   chart renders each as JSON into a ConfigMap the manager mounts read-only, with the security
   context, egress gate, credentials, volumes, colocation and labels their policies select.
   - A template exists only once its image is named; until then the manager journals the start,
     and the start fails until the image exists.
   - While the manager is rendered, the runtime and executor render as no Deployment of their own:
     the manager starts every agent process itself (design §3.4).
   - The contract with lane P: the pod runs `mandate-daemon run --probe /tmp/process.sock` and
     reads its identity from `MANDATE_WORKSPACE`, `MANDATE_PROCESS`, `MANDATE_SUBJECT` and
     `MANDATE_GENERATION`. It resolves the rest from the journal and refuses, before it is ready,
     anything it cannot match.

8. **A pod is selectable by its subject, and the chart's labels are kept.** The adapter adds the
   label `mandate.dev/subject-key`, the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the subject, and
   `mandate.dev/workspace-key`, the same of the workspace. It selects its own pods by that key and
   `app.kubernetes.io/managed-by`.
   - The template's `mandate.dev/workspace` label stays as the chart wrote it. The network policies,
     the colocation affinity and the model gateway's ingress select by it. Slice 1 overwrote it,
     which would have left every started pod outside its egress policy.
   - The manager reads the hosted workspace's ULID (`api.workspace`), whose control stream is
     `ctl:{workspace_id}`, never the release's label.
   An agent id or `account_ref` is not always label-safe, so the subject itself stays the
   `mandate.dev/subject` annotation. The demo selects the agent's pods by process and subject key.

9. **Until a deployment names its connection on the journal ([DEC-1171](DEC-1171.md)), the
   process's resolver knows no account, so the manager starts nothing for a deployment.** That
   fails closed. A kill switch is never delayed by it: kill switches never ask the resolver. The
   next change reads the account from `AgentDeployed` version 2 and drops the resolver.

### Rationale

Each item gives one pod exactly what its job needs, and checks it where a later change could widen
it:
- The Role names one resource and four verbs. Because creating a pod is itself powerful, the
  admission policy shapes every pod the manager makes like the chart's templates: their images,
  their Secrets, their labels and egress, no token, nothing privileged and nothing of the host.
- The egress policy names one destination, for one pod.
- The gate keeps proving the filter before anything runs.
- The chart's invariant tests fail on a widened Role, a second pod with a token or with `kube-api`
  egress, and a gate that probes the API server again. They also fail on a missing or ignoring
  admission policy, a match condition naming another account, a widened image or Secret list, and a
  dropped bound. They check each template as the manager starts it passes the policy's bounds, and
  that a pod mounting another role's Secret, claiming another process's egress, or running
  privileged does not.

The kill switch's path stays as design §3.6 lists it. Its running processes read the switch
themselves, and the manager only starts a covered process that is down.

### Alternatives considered

- **A ClusterRole, or a Role over every resource the chart renders.** Rejected: the manager only
  creates, reads and deletes bare pods in its own namespace.
- **Turning the egress gate off for the manager's pod.** Rejected: until the policy controller
  programs the pod's filter, the pod reaches anything (AGENTS.md rule 3). Probing a denied
  destination keeps that proof.
- **A Compose adapter now.** Left to a later change: the demo runs on k3d only, and the Compose
  file holds no manager.
- **Passing the connection and owner to the runtime as flags.** Rejected with lane P: the manager
  would have to read the mandate document, which the cell has nowhere shared to store. The runtime
  resolves them from the journal and refuses on any mismatch.

### Consequences

- The cell's demo can observe `ProcessStartIssued` for an agent's runtime and executor once
  [DEC-1171](DEC-1171.md)'s reading lands. It selects their pods by `mandate.dev/process` and
  `mandate.dev/subject-key`.
- Left to later changes:
  - a Compose adapter;
  - alerts through the cell's alert path rather than stderr;
  - alerts for stuck `Pending` or `Unknown` pods;
  - deleting exited pods;
  - the restart drill through `cargo xtask cell drill`;
  - an owner's resume lifting a crash-loop pause, after E21-30 copies the pause.

<a id="dec-1176"></a>

## DEC-1176: A dev or ci cell serves the fixture OAuth provider from its own pod, gated by tier

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: a reversible engineering decision under DEC-79 and DEC-176, approved by the coordinator and agreed by lane GO on 2026-10-19; it renders only in dev and ci cells and adds no reach to staging or production) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-19 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (lane D1, the staging push), with lanes GO and X, under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | DEC-2625 (the fixture OAuth provider), DEC-2100 (the stub issuer on loopback), DEC-2633 (the exchange's cell token URL), DEC-2627, DEC-1175; claim #1901 |

### Context

The OAuth connect's cell drill needs the token-exchange pod, and the owner's browser, to reach the
fixture OAuth provider (DEC-2625, `POST /alpaca/oauth/token`). The provider is served by the stub
issuer, a sidecar of the API pod bound to loopback only (DEC-2100), so no other pod can reach it.
This is for dev and ci cells alone; staging reaches Alpaca's own token host (DEC-2059).

### Decision

1. **Its own pod.** A dev or ci cell renders a one-replica `oauth-fixture` Deployment from the stub
   issuer's image, run as `mandate-stub-issuer --alpaca-only --bind 0.0.0.0:8091 --public-host
   oauth-fixture:8091` with the provider's three flags (item 8): it serves only `/alpaca/oauth/*`
   and answers 404 to everything else, so no sign-in issuer runs in it. A ClusterIP Service `oauth-fixture` fronts it on 8091. The API pod's sidecar stays on
   loopback, unchanged.
2. **Gated on the tier alone.** Any staging or production render that names the fixture or sets
   any of its values fails, whether or not `images.stub-issuer` is named, and the values schema
   refuses it there. A test renders `deploy/gitops/clusters/{staging,production}` and finds no
   `oauth-fixture` Deployment, Service, or NetworkPolicy.
3. **It reaches nothing.** Its NetworkPolicy has `policyTypes` Ingress and Egress and no egress
   rule, not even DNS: the fixture never calls out.
4. **Only the exchange and the browser reach it.** Its ingress admits pods labelled
   `mandate.dev/process: token-exchange` in this workspace on 8091 and nothing else: no
   namespace-wide ingress. The local cell runs no ingress controller (Traefik is disabled), so the
   browser reaches the fixture as it reaches the API, through `kubectl port-forward` to the
   `oauth-fixture` Service on 8091 with `oauth-fixture` resolved to loopback, and so sends the one
   `Host` the fixture answers (item 8); a port-forward is not pod-network ingress, and the policy
   admits no other pod. In dev and ci only, the exchange's `token-host` egress renders as a
   pod selector on `oauth-fixture` port 8091, with no CIDR beside it; in staging and production it
   stays the `token-host` CIDR path of the values, and the fixture's selector never appears there.
5. **The exchange's target.** The token-exchange client's host is Alpaca's
   (`https://api.alpaca.markets`, DEC-821 item 4, DEC-2059). A base-URL override pointing it at
   `http://oauth-fixture:8091/alpaca/oauth/token` is a dev and ci value only, the exchange pod's
   `MANDATE_TOKEN_URL`: the schema refuses it, and the chart fails, under staging or production.
   The binary reads it only in a build with debug assertions, and there only when
   `MANDATE_DEPLOYMENT_TIER` is dev or ci and the origin is loopback or exactly
   `http://oauth-fixture:8091`, scheme, host and port pinned (DEC-2633, which widens DEC-2629 by that
   one origin; `MANDATE_ALPACA_AUTHORIZE_URL` takes the same gate); a release image that is handed it
   refuses to start. A stray value can then never repoint a staging or production exchange.
6. **The fixture's client.** The Secret `oauth-fixture-client` holds the fixture client's fake
   secret under the key `client-secret`. The chart makes no Secret (DEC-822 item 4 holds as it
   is): `cargo xtask cell up` makes it once in the local cell, before the workspace release, from
   32 random bytes written as hex and sent to kubectl on stdin, and keeps one already there, so the
   pod and the vault agree across every `cell up`. The chart only mounts it, read-only, mode 0440
   for the pod's fsGroup as every Secret here is, and an init container copies it to a
   memory-backed directory as 0600, which is the fixture's `--alpaca-client-secret-file`, since the
   stub refuses a file any group can read (DEC-2625 item 2), as the journal role's `pgpass` is
   copied. After the release is installed, `cell up` seeds the same value into the vault at the
   platform client secret's path (`SecretPath::ClientSecret`, `platform/alpaca-oauth-client-secret`),
   so the exchange presents what the fixture expects; it does nothing while the Secret is absent
   (lane V2, #2197). The client id is `alpacaOAuth.clientId`, the one value the API and the exchange
   send. Nothing is generated at the fixture's start, which would need the fixture and the vault
   kept in step.
7. **What is tested.** Each of items 2 to 6 is pinned by a chart test (`deploy/tests/test_oauth_fixture.py`,
   and `test_gitops.py` for the staging and production clusters), with a seeded bug for each: the
   tier gate with and without the image and with the schema skipped, an egress rule on the
   fixture, a CIDR beside the fixture's selector, the token URL repointed or present with the
   fixture off, an ingress rule wider than the exchange's pods, a client Secret the chart makes, and
   the client Secret mounted or copied with wider bits; xtask's tests pin that `cell up` makes the
   Secret before the release and seeds the vault after it.
8. **The stub issuer's fixture mode** (`mandate-stub-issuer`, lane D1 with lane X's agreement).
   `--alpaca-only` runs the fixture provider without the issuer: no signing key is made, and
   `GET /jwks.json` and `POST /token` answer 404 like any other path outside `/alpaca/oauth/*`. It
   needs the provider's three flags (else `stub_issuer_alpaca`) and `--public-host`, the one
   `Host` it answers, 421 to any other or to two, as the issuer answers for its bind (else
   `stub_issuer_public_host`). `--public-host` is a lowercase host name, a dot-separated list of
   labels of letters, digits and inner hyphens, a colon, and a port from 1 to 65535, written
   without a leading zero; nothing else, no scheme, path or user. Only in this mode may `--bind`
   name an address that is not loopback, and only with a port from 1 to 65535 that prints back as
   written, so the pod can listen on its own address. `--public-host` without `--alpaca-only` is
   refused (`stub_issuer_public_host`), so the issuer's own sidecar stays on loopback (DEC-2100
   item 5). The tier and build checks run first, as for the issuer (DEC-2100 items 2 and 3): a
   release build and a tier other than dev or ci still refuse before anything is read.

### Rationale

The fixture runs the provider without the issuer, on a pod that reaches nothing and that only the
exchange and the browser reach, and it exists only where no real money or account does. Keying the
gate on the tier, not on whether an image is named, means no combination of staging values can
render it.

### Alternatives considered

- **Relax the sidecar's bind behind the tier gate.** Rejected: it would expose the API pod's whole
  stub surface, sign-in included, to the namespace.
- **Run the drill against Alpaca's paper OAuth.** Rejected for the cell: it needs a registered
  client and the network, and the cell runs with neither (ADR-0001 ES-19).

<a id="dec-1177"></a>

## DEC-1177: How the deployment manager's token exchange is admitted, launched and read

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: reversible engineering decisions under DEC-79 and DEC-176; each narrows what the deployment manager may create or delete, or fixes how it runs the one-shot exchange DEC-1175 admits, and none touches an order, a limit, or an exit); amended by [DEC-2332](trading-core.md#dec-2332): how an existing or orphaned account is treated, the environment pin, the vault CA and the exchange's launch |
| **Date** | 2026-10-22 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | `claude-code` (lane D1, the staging push), under DEC-79 and DEC-176; number assigned by the coordinator |
| **Story or claim** | DEC-2627 items 1 and 5; E21-33; DEC-1175; DEC-2060; infrastructure design §3.4, §3.5; the chart and security reviews of #2077 |

### Context

DEC-1175 lets the deployment manager start an OAuth connect's token exchange once, as a bare pod,
and stop it when its request closes. The exchange is the one process that reaches the live token
host, and it logs in to the vault as its connection's own ServiceAccount (DEC-2060). This decision
fixes what the chart admits for it, what the manager may create and delete, how the Kubernetes
adapter launches it, and how the manager reads its request. DEC-1175 is Accepted and its text does
not change; this file carries what came after it.

### Decision

1. **The admission pin is per process.** The deploy-manager's pod policy reads the pod's
   `mandate.dev/process` label and holds it to that process's template alone: its images; exactly
   its containers' commands and arguments, the container count included; its Secrets (none for the
   exchange); its read-only claims; and its projected volume. A token exchange runs as an `x`-and-hex
   account, every other process as `default`. The exchange's process entry is `one_shot`: never a
   standing Deployment, it runs its pinned launch with no probe.
2. **Each policy literal holds one CEL type.** The API server's CEL checker refuses a map or list
   literal that mixes types, which a Python mirror of the rules could not see (#2077's first cell
   run). Each launch is `[command, args]` by container name, each projected volume one string
   (`audience|seconds|path|mode|item=field`), and the account check uses plain equalities. A test
   checks that every policy variable keeps one type at each depth.
3. **The tests evaluate the rendered CEL.** `deploy/tests/test_cell.py` evaluates each policy's own
   expressions with cel-python (`cel-python==0.5.0`, in the Python workspace's dev group;
   `docs/dependencies.md`), as the API server evaluates them, over each template as the manager
   starts it and each attack: a rule that is dropped or loosened, `|| true` included, or a list that
   is widened, admits an attack and fails the tests. cel-python asks for PyYAML 6.0.2 or later, which
   only adds wheels, so a uv override keeps the workspace's 6.0.1 (the reference's). The same pods
   and attacks were applied to a k3s API server as the manager's own account, and it agreed with
   cel-python on every case it reached admission for.
4. **The manager makes and deletes the exchange's account, bounded.** Its Role creates, gets, lists
   and deletes `serviceaccounts` in its namespace. A second policy, over create, update and delete
   alike (a delete reads the account as it stands, `oldObject`), refuses any account not named as
   DEC-2060 names an exchange's, `x` and an even count of lowercase hex digits, at most 129
   characters, so neither `default` nor the manager's own account; one that mounts a token or holds
   a Secret or pull secret; and one without the manager's, this workspace's and the
   `token-exchange` labels. The manager copies the workspace label from the process's template, the
   chart's value, never derived from the workspace id.
5. **An executor's account comes with the change that runs the executor as it.** The pod policy
   cannot pin an executor pod to its own `<hex>` account unless the same change makes the adapter
   name and create that account, which needs the executor's connection, and the executor slot is
   keyed by `account_ref`. Pinning it alone would refuse the executor pods the manager makes today.
   So, for now, executor pods stay on `default` and the accounts policy admits the exchange's form
   only; the change that starts the executor for a connect (DEC-2627 item 5) brings the executor's
   account, its adapter and manager code and its pod-policy pin together.
6. **The exchange's account is named in its environment.** CEL cannot hex the pod's subject, so
   the manager sets `MANDATE_SUBJECT_ACCOUNT`, the account it computed, beside `MANDATE_SUBJECT`. The
   pod policy requires each of the exchange's containers to name each exactly once, the account
   equal to the pod's `serviceAccountName`; the exchange refuses to start unless that account is `x`
   and the hex of `MANDATE_SUBJECT`'s bytes. So the process that reaches the live token host cannot
   log in as another connection's role short of its own binary being wrong.
7. **No root, no added capability, no chosen node.** Every pod the manager makes sets
   `runAsNonRoot`, names no uid 0 for the pod or any container, adds no capability, and names no
   host alias or node. The three templates already hold this.
8. **The projected volume is exactly the vault token and the namespace.** A `vault_token` process
   mounts one projected volume, mode 0444, read-only at `/run/mandate/vault-token`: the token
   (audience `mandate-vault`, 600 seconds, `token`), then one downward item, `metadata.namespace` at
   `namespace`, with no mode of its own. The pod policy refuses any other source, order, field,
   item or mode.
9. **The launch is at the image root.** Release images put each binary at `/<name>`
   (`xtask/src/image.rs`), so the exchange runs `/mandate-token-exchange` with no argument, reading
   its connection from `MANDATE_SUBJECT`, and the chart's tests refuse a container whose launch names
   a `mandate-` binary anywhere else.
10. **When the exchange starts.** The manager starts it on the request itself, after creating its
    account; no record says the vault role exists. The exchange's login retries a missing role or an
    unreachable vault with a short capped backoff until its deadline, and stops at once on any other
    refusal (DEC-2060's recommendation).
11. **How the adapter runs it.** The orchestrator's start takes the `lifetime` the request has left,
    for a token exchange only, and a standing process never one. The pod's `activeDeadlineSeconds`
    is that lifetime in whole seconds, rounded down, and no exchange is created with less than a
    second left or with none. Before the pod the adapter makes the account (point 4), keeping one
    that already exists, and asks for no pod until it does. A stop deletes the account, then the
    pod, so a stop that fails leaves the pod listed and the manager asks again; either already gone
    is done. The process label, template file and pod name use the chart's spelling
    (`token-exchange`, `tx`); the journal and `MANDATE_PROCESS` keep `token_exchange`. The deadline
    counts from the start's journaling and the pod's from when the kubelet takes it, so a pod can
    outlive its request by its scheduling delay: the code it would redeem has expired by then, and
    the vault grant's own 600 seconds still bound it.
12. **How the manager reads a request.** A version-2 `ConnectionRequested` with `credential`
    `oauth` wants its connection's exchange until a record closes it: a `ConnectionEstablished` of
    either version, a `ConnectionRefused` with occasion `connect`, or a `ConnectionRevoked`. A
    closing record read before the request also closes it. The exchange's lifetime is the request's
    `recorded_at` plus 600 seconds less the pass's time. A start journaled but not seen carried out
    is asked of the orchestrator again only while that lifetime is a second or more, and only by
    the manager that journaled it: after a restart it counts as the one attempt. Once the request
    closes, the manager stops every instance still listed, exited or not, so its pod and account
    are deleted.

### Rationale

Each point narrows what a compromised or careless manager can create: another process's image,
launch, credentials or account under the exchange's label, a root or privileged pod, an account
that is not a connection's exchange's, or the deletion of one that is not. Evaluating the policies'
own CEL in the tests makes a loosened rule fail, where a mirror of it passed. Keeping the executor's
account for the change that runs the executor as it adds no account nothing uses.

### Alternatives considered

- **Keep the Python mirror of each rule.** Rejected (the security review of #2077): `|| true` on a
  rule passed it.
- **Compare each rendered expression with a literal.** Rejected while a pinned evaluator works
  behind the proxy: it checks the text, not what the text admits.
- **Pin executor pods to `<hex>` now.** Deferred (point 5): it would refuse today's executor pods.
- **Check the account's hex in CEL.** Not possible: CEL has no byte-to-hex function, so the
  exchange checks it (point 6).

<a id="dec-1179"></a>

## DEC-1179: The fixture's token refusals are written to standard error as their code and cause alone

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (agent: a reversible engineering decision under DEC-79 and DEC-176. It changes only the stub issuer, which runs only where `MANDATE_DEPLOYMENT_TIER` is `dev` or `ci` and is compiled out of every build without debug assertions; the answers on the wire do not change, and no safety rule is weakened) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-25 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | an agent, under DEC-79 (`claude-code`, lane D1, claim [#1901](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1901)) |
| **Story or claim** | E10-23; [DEC-2625](DEC-2625.md) items 4 and 6; [DEC-2100](DEC-2100.md) item 7; `AGENTS.md` rule 7 |

### Context

In the cell's connect drill, the token-exchange process reports `endpoint_failed` against the
fixture provider's `POST /alpaca/oauth/token`, and nothing says why. The fixture answers RFC 6749
§5.2's `error` alone, and `invalid_grant` covers four different checks (an unknown code, an expired
code, another redirect URI, another verifier). The exchange process may not log the answer's body,
and the fixture logged nothing, so a refused exchange could not be told from any other.

### Decision

1. **The line.** For each refusal of `POST /alpaca/oauth/token`, the fixture writes one line to
   standard error: `token_refused`, the answer's `error`, and the word of the check that refused it,
   separated by single spaces. The words are `form` (not exactly DEC-2625 item 4's form, or not
   UTF-8), `grant_type`, `client` (another client id or secret), `code_unknown`, `code_expired`,
   `redirect_uri`, `code_verifier`, and `store_full` (the token store is full or the random source
   failed). For example: `token_refused invalid_grant redirect_uri`.
2. **Before the provider.** A body that is not a form is written as `token_refused
   unsupported_media_type`, and one over the body limit as `token_refused payload_too_large`.
3. **No request value.** A line holds only those fixed words: never the client id or secret, the
   code, the redirect URI, the verifier, a token, a header, or any other value the request carried
   (`AGENTS.md` rule 7). A test asserts the lines byte for byte, and that none of a refused
   request's values appears in them.
4. **The wire is unchanged.** The status and body each refusal answers stay DEC-2625 item 4's. The
   checks run in the same order, so a request that fails two checks is written with the first, as
   it is answered.
5. **Nothing else.** Issued tokens, the authorize route, and the account route write nothing, and
   standard output keeps the announce line alone.

### Rationale

The cause is what a drill needs to tell a race (the code not yet written, or spent) from a
misconfiguration (another redirect URI or client secret). Fixed words carry the cause without
carrying a credential. The stub runs only in `dev` and `ci` and holds only the fixture's own
client, so naming which check failed discloses nothing a production provider would hide.

### Alternatives considered

- **A separate `error` code per cause on the wire.** Rejected: the fixture answers as Alpaca's
  provider does, and RFC 6749 §5.2 fixes the codes.
- **Logging the request with its secrets redacted.** Rejected: a redaction that misses a field
  leaks it, and fixed words cannot.
- **Logging in the token-exchange process instead.** Rejected for this change: the exchange sees
  only the `error`, which does not separate the four `invalid_grant` checks.

<a id="dec-1190"></a>

## DEC-1190: per-PR CI jobs scoped to the diff's affected crates, everything nightly on main

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (items 1 to 4 and 6); item 5's rotation Proposed (founder) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The founder chose the direction in the coordinator's session (2026-10-17, about 06:30 UTC). An agent (Claude Code, lane CI-R) wrote the build of it under DEC-79 and DEC-176, taking the strict reading throughout. Item 5's seven-run rotation is the one place the build departs from the founder's wording, so it waits for the founder |
| **Story or claim** | E1-9, claim #1481 |

### The founder's choice

Asked to choose between "Gate by risk (Recommended)", "Gate by risk + bigger runners" and "Make CI
optional", the founder chose **"Gate by risk (Recommended)"**, worded as: "PRs that touch
safety-critical crates (risk, executor, accounting, vault, auth, connectors, journal) keep the full
required checks and mutants. Docs, specs, web, charts and tooling PRs run only fast lint and tests;
mutants and the heavy matrix skip them. Full mutation sweep nightly on main for everything."

About seventeen builders share GitHub's free runner pool (20 concurrent jobs, 5 of them macOS), and
finished pull requests waited 30 to 60 minutes. The measurement behind this decision, from 17 recent
`ci` runs, is in the pull request that records it: per non-documentation PR the non-mutant jobs cost about 42 runner-minutes over
11 to 16 jobs. The mutation shards cost a median of 103 runner-minutes, over a median of 65 jobs
when a diff has mutants. Job waits of 8 to 20 minutes came from the pool's concurrency, not from
slow jobs.

### Decision

1. **The strict reading.** A job is skipped for a diff only when nothing in the diff can change its
   verdict. When the selection is in doubt it selects more. The mutation gate on the diff of
   safety-critical crates is unchanged (DEC-538, DEC-519, DEC-931), and so are lint, the spec guard
   and the trailer check. The founder's wording names crates by area. The build goes further: any
   crate whose code or data a diff can reach keeps every check that could fail on it, whether or not
   it is safety-critical.
2. **The affected set** (`cargo xtask ci select`, `select` in `xtask`). The selection takes:
   - the changed paths against the base `choose_base` picks (DEC-112's base, with renames as both
     sides);
   - each path's owning crate;
   - every crate whose Rust sources name the path. The scan reads every string literal piece, a
     leading `/` dropped, resolved against the file's directory, the crate's directory, `crates/`
     and the repository root. So `include_str!`, `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR` joins, `concat!(env!(..),
     "/../..")` pieces, a sibling crate named from a computed `crates` directory, format strings
     and globs all count. It drops only a directory above the crate's own (`crates`), because `..`
     pieces in path-traversal tests would otherwise make every crate read every crate. A test
     pins the scan against an independent reading: every tracked outside path any run of path
     characters in a crate's code names, read without the tokenizer, must be one the scan covers;
   - every crate whose sources name the variable through which a test is handed another crate's
     binary (`MANDATE_RH_SIM_BIN`: `mandate-mcp`'s rehearsal tests run `mandate-rh-sim`'s
     simulator without depending on it), whenever that crate is selected;
   - every workspace member depending on one of those, directly or not, through normal, dev or
     build dependencies.

   Tests (nextest, sharded as DEC-871), doctests, pending tests (DEC-110, DEC-137), the `rh-sim`
   rehearsal runs (DEC-963) and the Postgres runs (DEC-520) cover only that set. The jobs follow it:

   | Job | Starts when |
   |---|---|
   | `fast-test`, `fast-pending` | the set is not empty. xtask's sources name `docs/`, `web/` and `crates`, so its own tests run on documentation and web changes too, which the founder's "docs… run only fast lint and tests" allows; they build only xtask |
   | `fast-python` | a path outside every crate, the documentation and `web/` changed |
   | `fast-portable` (DEC-954), `fast-rh-sim-live` (DEC-964) | the set holds the runner |
   | `full-checks` | a path outside the documentation and `web/` changed (DEC-112's classification, which a test pins to `docs-only.sh`) |
   | `schema-mutants` (DEC-688) | `schemas/`, the specs (the journal spec among them, since `check_planned.py` reads every spec), the backlog, or `reference/mandate/requirements.txt` changed |

   No workspace member turns on another member's feature or turns its defaults off, so a
   `--package` build unifies each selected crate's features as the `--workspace` build does: every
   binary containing a crate is in the set and brings its own dependencies' features. If a member
   ever does turn on another's feature, the selection takes everything.
3. **Everything.** The following select every crate and every job:
   - a change to `Cargo.lock`, any `Cargo.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `deny.toml`, `clippy.toml`,
     `.cargo/`, `.config/`, `.github/`, `xtask/` (`layers.toml` included), `.gitignore` or
     `.python-version`;
   - a path outside every crate and outside the known top-level paths;
   - a run with no diff base (main, the nightly).
4. **The required checks.** `fast` and `full` keep their names, so branch protection and the merge
   gate (DEC-175) are unchanged. Each passes only when `select` succeeded, each part it selected
   succeeded, and each part it did not select was skipped. A skip `select` did not ask for fails,
   and so does a job that ran when it should not have. A skipped job starts no runner. The
   aggregates print the selection's one-line reasons in their logs and job summaries, and `select`
   writes them to its own summary. `cancel-in-progress` already cancels a superseded run of the
   same pull request (DEC-610).
5. **The nightly on main** (`nightly.yml`, every night at 06:17 UTC; DEC-256 item 3 made it weekly
   to save private-repository minutes, and the repository is public again).
   - `dispatch-ci` dispatches `ci.yml` on main. That run has no diff base, so it selects
     everything: all tests, `schema-mutants`, and the macOS `portable` job. A local reusable
     workflow cannot be used, because the live-feature check refuses `uses: ./…` (DEC-873 item 6).
     When that run fails, `ci.yml`'s `report-main` opens an issue. A run cancelled by a newer dispatch
     opens none.
   - The nightly itself runs the Postgres tests on PostgreSQL 17, ten fuzz seeds, the reference
     implementation's seeded-bug check (`mutants.py`), and a full-history gitleaks scan.
   - It also runs the **whole-crate mutation sweep** of every safety-critical crate (`cargo xtask ci
     sweep`). The sweep is the per-PR gate over a diff that adds every source line of those crates.
     Each shard is judged by the tests of the crates its own mutants are in, plus their external and
     simulator oracles, which is what a PR touching only those crates gets.
   - Shards hold at most eight mutants, the size DEC-473 found to fit ten minutes. Each day takes
     the shards whose index is the day (days since the Unix epoch, from the runner's clock, an
     input under ES-05) modulo the rotation, at most eight at a time. The sweep runs every night
     even when main is unchanged, so no day's share is skipped.
   - The rotation is seven days and lengthens on its own once a seventh would no longer fit one
     256-job matrix.
   - Any failed or cancelled job opens an issue naming only the commit, the run's address and each
     job's verdict.

   **Why the rotation, and why it is Proposed.** The founder's words are "full mutation sweep
   nightly". The sweep lists 12,538 mutants today, and they take about 11 to 13 seconds each after
   a baseline (measured on `mandate-risk`). A full sweep every night would be roughly 45 to 50
   runner-hours, holding the whole 20-runner pool for hours, which defeats the reason for this
   decision. A seventh a night is about 1,800 mutants and some 224 shards, about 12 runner-hours,
   and about an hour and a half of wall-clock at eight in parallel. Every mutant is still tested
   once a week.

   The rotation removes nothing that runs today. On main the old nightly's `mutants` step had no
   base and so swept nothing, and the per-PR gate is unchanged. But it is weaker than the
   founder's words, so it stays Proposed. The founder can set `SWEEP_ROTATION` to 1 for a full
   nightly sweep, and `max-parallel` bounds the pool either way. The first run is expected to open
   an issue: on a local sample the sweep already found a mutant no PR diff ever selected, in
   `mandate-risk`'s `ReasonCode` `Display`.
6. **DEC-464, which DEC-473 and DEC-498 superseded, is honoured as written.** Its rule is to add
   shards rather than remove tests, baselines, mutants or safety gates. This decision removes
   nothing; it scopes per diff. A job the diff cannot affect does not start, and every job still
   runs for every diff that reaches it and for main every night. Every per-PR job keeps its
   ten-minute cap, as do the nightly's jobs. `cargo xtask check` is scoped the same way, so a
   local run judges what CI judges. With no base, on main, it runs everything.

### Rationale

The queue comes from concurrency: each job holds a runner slot, so a job that cannot change the
verdict costs another PR its turn. Scoping by the workspace graph and by what the sources read
keeps each check wherever it could fail.

Several properties are tested:

- The selection always holds every reverse dependent of a changed crate: a seeded property test
  checks it against Warshall's closure, an oracle computed another way.
- A change to a dependency alone still catches a crate it breaks: a fixture workspace's scoped run
  fails, and a changed-crates-only selector misses it.
- The verdict scripts are judged over their truth tables, as DEC-538's and DEC-871's tests do.

The scan also closes a gap DEC-112 left. Tests read `docs/specs/identity.md`, `workspace-api.md`
and `mandate.md`, so a documentation-only change could break them unseen until the next code PR.
Now it selects their crates.

Replaying the selector over 49 recent main commits:

- 14 select everything (a lock file, a manifest or CI changed).
- `schema-mutants` is skipped on 25, `fast-python` on 20, `fast-portable` and `fast-rh-sim-live` on
  6 each, and `full-checks` on 5. Tests always run, since xtask's own are selected, but on the
  other 35 they cover a subset of the 48 crates.
- Counting only whole-job skips, the non-mutant work drops from about 40.5 to 34.6 runner-minutes
  and from 11 to 9.7 jobs per PR. The lighter builds of scoped test runs come on top.

The largest lever, the mutation shards (median 65 jobs of about one minute each, mostly setup,
when a diff has mutants), is left as it is by the founder's rule. Packing several mutants a shard
would cut those slots several times over and is proposed separately.

### Alternatives considered

- **Select by crate area only** (the founder's list of safety-critical areas). Not taken: a crate
  outside the list can break one inside it through a dependency, and a spec can break a crate's
  tests. The graph and the scan decide instead.
- **Keep starting every job and let it exit early.** Not taken: the job still holds a runner slot
  for its setup, which is the queue cost this decision removes.
- **A reusable workflow for the nightly.** Refused by the live-feature check (DEC-873 item 6).
  Dispatching `ci.yml` reuses the per-PR jobs exactly.
- **A full whole-crate sweep every night.** Measured at about 45 to 50 runner-hours a night; see
  item 5.

<a id="dec-1200"></a>

## DEC-1200: The workspace data service's first slice: how a quote becomes, stays, and stops being the mark

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Accepted (by the agent, DEC-79 and DEC-176: each item is a reversible engineering reading that only withholds a quote from openings, never adds risk and never touches an exit) |
| **Date** | 2026-10-17 (UTC) |
| **Decided by** | The agent (`claude-code`, the lane M2 builder), under DEC-79 and DEC-176 |
| **Story or claim** | E2-6, claim [#1453](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/1453); [data-plane spec](../../specs/data-plane.md) v0.2 §3.3, §3.4, §5.1; DEC-433 items 5 to 8 |

### Context

E2-6 builds the workspace data service: spec §3.1's normalized records with `vendor_time` and
`received_at`, and a bounded hot cache that every runtime and the executor read. The gate already
takes `MarketSnapshot.quote: Option<SaneQuote>` and leaves the staleness threshold to the reader
("a quote older than the profile's threshold never reaches the gate: the fold supplies `None`",
`mandate-risk`'s `usable_quote`, DEC-163 item 1). The data service is that reader. The spec says
what must hold; it leaves a few mechanics open, and these items pick the reading that hands the
gate no quote whenever it is in doubt.

### Decision

1. **The crate and its slices.** The service is `mandate-dataservice` (safety-critical, layer 8,
   above `mandate-alpaca`, whose stream messages it will normalize, and `mandate-risk`, whose
   `SaneQuote` it produces). Under ES-13's limit E2-6 lands in slices: (1) quotes and the hot
   cache, this decision; (2) instrument status and LULD bands (§3.5); (3) the bounded push queues
   with conflation and the subscription set's priority and overflow report (§3.2, §3.6); (4) the
   Alpaca stream messages normalized from recorded fixtures; (5) the journal spec change that adds
   vendor and receive times to `MarkUpdated` (§11 question 2), in its own PR first (ES-22).
2. **"The last accepted quote" of DEC-433 item 8 is the order watermark:** the last quote, per
   instrument and feed, that was in order and not future-stamped, whether or not it was sane. A
   quote stamped at or before it never marks. A crossed or too-wide quote in order moves the
   watermark and withdraws the mark, so an older sane quote arriving after it cannot mark.
3. **A future-stamped quote withdraws the mark and does not move the watermark.** Its vendor time
   is not trusted, so it cannot hold back later quotes as out of order; but the instrument has no
   mark until a later sane, in-order quote.
4. **Quote age counts UTC nanoseconds**, from the earlier of the two times (DEC-433 item 5), not
   regular-session time. Wall time is never shorter than session time, so a quote is stale at least
   as soon. This governs only the quote handed to openings; the gate's `stale_mark` restriction,
   which never flattens, keeps its own clock (mandate spec §5.2).
5. **Resync is an instant, not a call order.** A subscription and a connection loss each carry
   the host-clock instant they happened at. A quote received at or before the later of the two
   never marks and moves neither the mark nor the watermark (`Intake::BeforeResync`), however late
   it is taken, so a quote still queued from a dead connection cannot mark after the loss (DP-7: a
   mark comes only from a live quote received after resync; spec §3.2, `resyncing`: a live quote
   received after the subscription). A loss also withdraws every current mark, and watermarks
   stay, so a replay of an earlier quote never restores one. A resubscription after an
   unsubscribe starts with no watermark, but takes nothing received before it. E2-5's connection
   states will call both.
6. **The spread limit is a fraction of the bid:** sane means `bid ≤ ask ≤ bid × (1 + max_spread)`,
   the bound rounded down (`Price::collar_bound`). Measured against the bid, the spread reads at
   least as wide as against the mid, so the limit binds no later.
7. **The host clock starts not known to be in tolerance.** No quote is fresh for an opening until
   the caller reports an in-tolerance clock (journal spec §5.4's `ClockOffsetRecorded`), and none
   while `ClockToleranceExceeded` stands (DEC-433 item 5). `AGENTS.md` rule 3: the unknown state is
   the safe one. Nothing reports it yet: until the service process wires the scheduler's clock
   events in (with E2-5 and E21-44), the cache hands no quote to an opening at all. The freshness
   check counts from the earlier of the two times and only asks that it be at or before `now`, so
   a receive time after `now` (a host clock stepped back) is this item's to catch, not freshness's.
8. **Duplicates in this slice are detected against the watermark only**; any other repeat is out
   of order and never marks either. The "never stored twice" half of §3.3 lands with the store.
9. **The cache is bounded by an explicit subscription set** with a capacity: a quote for an
   instrument not subscribed, or on another feed, is refused; subscribing past the capacity is
   refused, so the instrument left out has no quote and openings in it are denied (§3.2). Which
   instruments come first is slice 3's.

### Rationale

Every item resolves a gap toward handing the gate no quote, which denies openings and leaves exits
untouched (rules 3 and 13, DP-4); `last_marked` keeps the last sane quote for exit pricing however
old (trading spec §8.2). None changes a spec, a gate rule or an envelope field, so DEC-176 lets the
agent take them.

### Alternatives considered

- **Only sane quotes move the watermark.** An older sane quote could then mark after a later
  crossed one, pricing an opening from before the market went bad. Rejected (item 2).
- **A future-stamped quote moves the watermark.** One bad stamp an hour ahead would make every real
  quote for the next hour out of order. Rejected (item 3); it stays safe either way, since both
  deny openings.
- **Count session time for openings.** The spec's session-time rule is for `stale_mark`'s
  restriction, which must not fire overnight; for an opening, wall time is the tighter reading.
- **Start with the clock in tolerance.** Rejected: nothing has shown it yet (item 7).
- **A connection loss as a call with no time** (this slice's first draft): a quote received before
  the loss but taken after it could mark. The independent review found it; rejected (item 5).
